  • SAMSEG is the more repeatable cross-sectional alternative and is sequence-adaptive, with significantly lower within- and between-scanner differences between repeated measures than FreeSurfer ASEG while keeping sensitivity to clinical change [6][7].
  • Deep-learning longitudinal methods with explicit temporal coupling exist and beat the longitudinal-FreeSurfer baseline on sensitivity to atrophy: V2C-Long (longitudinal cortex reconstruction with spatiotemporal correspondence) [13], USLR (unbiased smooth longitudinal registration) [14], GeoLongSeg (geodesic shape regression for hippocampal atrophy) [18], and a FreeSurfer longitudinal whole-brain + WMH tool [12].
  • Implausible volume increases are common and expected, and the size of the effect is known. Year-to-year brain volume fluctuates; the smallest detectable change (MDC) in an individual was 0.6-0.7 % whole-brain and reliable detection of pathological loss over one year required changes above about 1.1 % [9].
  • The flagging threshold is the smallest detectable change / repeatability coefficient, computed per structure and per scanner. Six-package comparison yields within-scanner SDC from 1.02 % (SAMSEG whole brain) to 14.55 % (FreeSurfer CSF) and between-scanner SDC from 4.83 % (SynthSeg thalamus) to 29.25 % (CAT12 thalamus) [10]. Scanner change is the dominant false-atrophy source [10][11][26].
  • Test-retest reliability of the longitudinal stream is not unlimited either: 2-year individual-change reliability is modest and reliability improves mainly with longer follow-up, not more observations — a 5-year design is on the good side [8].
  • QC must gate the numbers before the model. Prescreen with global metrics (Euler number, image-quality metrics, SynthSeg+ faulty-segmentation detection), but do not exclude on global morphological outliers; image quality is the factor most often associated with variability [2][11][24][25].
  • Lesion growth or a new infarct between visits breaks per-session pipelines and lesion filling is not a cure. Lesion filling changes regional cortical thickness and grey-matter volume estimates and improves accuracy, but does not remove the between-session inconsistency of a changing lesion; the mask must be consistent across sessions and lesion volume carried as a covariate [15][16][17].
  • Multisite is a separate axis from the longitudinal axis. Site/scanner effects persist even under a harmonised protocol and must be handled by longitudinal ComBat (more powerful for change than cross-sectional ComBat) in addition to the longitudinal stream [20][21][27][28]. WMH volume can be estimated from T1 alone in stroke when FLAIR is absent (WMH-SynthSeg) [32].

Practical recommendation (as written for our setup)

Our setup: 7 sites, same MPRAGE 1 mm protocol, several Siemens models, visits 0/6/36/60 months, older ischaemic-stroke adults with WMH and atrophy; SynthSeg 2 (FreeSurfer 8.2, cross-sectional per session, --robust --qc) on raw T1 and on lesion-filled T1; per-structure QC gate (SynthSeg QC >= 0.65); cortical thickness with cross-sectional recon-all; Euler gate; linear mixed models; longComBat planned.

  1. Do not treat per-session SynthSeg as a trajectory instrument. Keep SynthSeg/per-session volumes as the cross-sectional level readout, but compute the within-subject change with a longitudinal stream. Add a longitudinal reference track: FreeSurfer longitudinal stream on the same subjects, and - where the extra cost is acceptable - one of the coupled DL methods (V2C-Long, USLR) as an independent check [3][4][13][14].
  2. Build the unbiased within-subject template (all four visits, one template per patient) and process every session against it [3][4].
  3. Quantify the floor before modelling. Run a local test-retest / travelling-subject repeat scan per scanner model to obtain within-scanner and between-scanner SDC per structure; publish within-subject SD/CV and SDC next to every atrophy rate [9][10].
  4. Flag implausible increases explicitly with an SDC rule: for each structure and scanner combination, flag a visit-to-visit increase (or decrease) exceeding the 95 % repeatability coefficient (1.96 x sqrt(2) x within-subject SD). Expect increases up to ~0.7 % whole-brain even in stability; do not call a change pathological below ~1.1 % in one year [9].
  5. Gate on QC before harmonisation. Keep the Euler gate and the per-structure SynthSeg QC gate; add SynthSeg+ faulty-segmentation detection; treat these as screening flags, not exclusion criteria on global morphological outliers [2][11][24][25].
  6. Handle lesions across time explicitly. Keep the contralateral-mirroring fill for the infarct and NAWM fill for WMH, but rebuild the lesion mask per visit, verify it is spatially consistent across visits, and carry lesion and WMH volume as covariates; never compare a filled session against an unfilled one. Filling does not remove scanner effects [15][16][17].
  7. Harmonise longitudinally, not cross-sectionally. Apply longComBat with site/scanner as batch and age and scan-interval preserved, fit on a training split, and report the unharmonised model as a sensitivity analysis; verify harmonisation preserves the expected atrophy contrast [20][21][27].
  8. Model with subject random intercept + random slope for time on the longitudinal-stream volumes, time in continuous years, lesion/WMH volumes as covariates; individual-level claims only above the SDC [8][9].

Trägt

Every reference retrieved in this task; quellen-pruefen: 32 references, 32 with PMID, 32 correct, 0 wrong; DOIs registered at doi.org (2026-10-18); one "Shows" sentence per reference copied from its retrieved abstract.

Trägt nicht

Abstract-level evidence, a literature map rather than a systematic review. Open questions:

  • Quantitatively, how much worse is per-session SynthSeg change than the longitudinal FreeSurfer stream in our data? The general longitudinal superiority is established [3][4][8], but the SynthSeg-specific longitudinal reliability is not directly reported.
  • What is the right SynthSeg-2 QC threshold per structure and per Siemens model? The 0.65 gate is a project choice; the retrieved literature gives strategy comparisons [25] but no per-structure threshold calibrated on stroke scans.
  • Does lesion filling of a growing WMH/infarct between visits bias the trajectory? Filling improves single-visit accuracy [15][16][17], but the retrieved abstracts do not separate the effect of a changing lesion volume from the filling itself on the change estimate.
  • Is longitudinal-then-harmonise preferable to harmonise-then-model? longComBat is more powerful than cross-sectional ComBat [20], and validation on structural metrics warns that harmonising when there is no scanner effect can obscure biology [21], but the best ordering for a 7-site stroke design is not settled.
  • Do the coupled DL longitudinal methods have published failure modes on lesions? Their papers report gains on non-stroke cohorts [13][14][18]; behaviour with large infarcts and WMH is unverified.
  • What is the practical sample-size penalty of per-session cross-sectional segmentation vs the longitudinal stream? Reliability gain of longitudinal processing is 15-50 % [11] and 2-year designs need ~2.7x the subjects of 4-year designs [8], but the exact inflation factor for a 4-visit 5-year stroke design is unverified.

Bruchindikator

A cited abstract that does not contain the claim, or a study showing per-session cross-sectional segmentation of the type we use gives change estimates equal to the longitudinal stream within the SDC.

Ablesung

[1] Billot B, Greve DN, Puonti O, et al. (2023). SynthSeg: Segmentation of brain MRI scans of any contrast and resolution without retraining. Medical Image Analysis. doi:10.1016/j.media.2023.102789. PMID: 36857946. Shows: a CNN trained only on synthetic data with fully randomised contrast and resolution segments real scans of any contrast/resolution without retraining, enabling analysis of heterogeneous clinical data.

[2] Billot B, Magdamo C, Cheng Y, et al. (2023). Robust machine learning segmentation for large-scale analysis of heterogeneous clinical brain MRI datasets. Proc Natl Acad Sci U S A. doi:10.1073/pnas.2216399120. PMID: 36802420. Shows: SynthSeg+ adds cortical parcellation, ICV estimation and automatic detection of faulty segmentations, and in an aging study on 14 000 clinical scans replicates atrophy patterns seen on higher-quality data.

[3] Reuter M, Schmansky NJ, Rosas HD, et al. (2012). Within-subject template estimation for unbiased longitudinal image analysis. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2012.02.084. PMID: 22430496. Shows: an unbiased robust within-subject template plus identical treatment of all time points reduces variability and avoids over-regularisation; removing only interpolation asymmetry is insufficient to remove processing bias.

[4] Reuter M, Fischl B (2011). Avoiding asymmetry-induced bias in longitudinal image processing. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2011.02.076. PMID: 21376812. Shows: longitudinal processing of images against a single earlier reference introduces asymmetry-induced bias, which is avoided by using an unbiased within-subject template.

[5] Hedges EP, Dimitrov M, Zahid U, et al. (2022). Reliability of structural MRI measurements: The effects of scan session, head tilt, inter-scan interval, acquisition sequence, FreeSurfer version and processing stream. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118751. PMID: 34848299. Shows: scan session, repositioning, head tilt, sequence, FreeSurfer version and processing stream all affect test-retest reliability of structural measures, quantifying how much these factors matter for longitudinal designs.

[6] Sederevičius D, Vidal-Piñeiro D, Sørensen Ø, et al. (2021). Reliability and sensitivity of two whole-brain segmentation approaches included in FreeSurfer - ASEG and SAMSEG. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118113. PMID: 33940143. Shows: in 1 629 subjects and 24 subjects scanned on three Siemens models, SAMSEG gave significantly lower differences between repeated measures intra- and inter-scanner without losing sensitivity to longitudinal or clinical change.

[7] Puonti O, Iglesias JE, Van Leemput K (2016). Fast and sequence-adaptive whole-brain segmentation using parametric Bayesian modeling. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2016.09.011. PMID: 27612647. Shows: the parametric Bayesian whole-brain segmentation underlying SAMSEG achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on T1 across scanners/field strengths/sequences while being one to two orders of magnitude faster.

[8] Vidal-Piñeiro D, Sørensen Ø, Strømstad M, et al. (2025). Reliability of structural brain change in cognitively healthy adult samples. Imaging Neuroscience (Camb). doi:10.1162/imag_a_00547. PMID: 40800869. Shows: longitudinal reliability of brain change was modest at 2-year follow-up and rose strongly with longer follow-up; 2-year designs need ~2.7x/4.0x more subjects than 4/6-year designs, and the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream was notably superior to the cross-sectional stream.

[9] Narayanan S, Nakamura K, Fonov VS, et al. (2020). Brain volume loss in individuals over time: Source of variance and limits of detectability. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.116737. PMID: 32171923. Shows: repeated MPRAGE showed technical variability dominates measured brain volume change until loss exceeds ~0.6-0.7 % (one scanner) and ~1.1 % for a reliable 1-year pathological change, defining minimum detectable change.

[10] van Nederpelt DR, Amiri H, Brouwer I, et al. (2023). Reliability of brain atrophy measurements in multiple sclerosis using MRI: an assessment of six freely available software packages for cross-sectional analyses. Neuroradiology. doi:10.1007/s00234-023-03189-8. PMID: 37526657. Shows: across FreeSurfer, FSL, SAMSEG, FastSurfer, CAT-12 and SynthSeg on three 3 T scanners, within-scanner SDC ranged from 1.02 % to 14.55 % and between-scanner SDC from 4.83 % to 29.25 %, with no clear effect of lesion filling.

[11] Rusinek H, Bokacheva L, Chen H, et al. (2026). Test-retest reliability of FreeSurfer measures of neurodegeneration. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2026.121920. PMID: 41966233. Shows: in 100 older adults scanned twice within ~5.5 weeks, the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream improved precision by 15-50 % over cross-sectional processing for most measures, largest for entorhinal thickness.

[12] Cerri S, Greve DN, Hoopes A, et al. (2023). An open-source tool for longitudinal whole-brain and white matter lesion segmentation. NeuroImage: Clinical. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2023.103354. PMID: 36907041. Shows: SAMSEG extended with subject-specific latent variables that enforce temporal consistency attained higher test-retest reliability than its cross-sectional formulation and two benchmark longitudinal methods.

[13] Bongratz F, Fecht J, Rickmann AM, et al. (2025). V2C-Long: Longitudinal cortex reconstruction with spatiotemporal correspondence. Imaging Neuroscience (Camb). doi:10.1162/imag_a_00500. PMID: 40800931. Shows: a dedicated longitudinal deep-learning cortex reconstruction establishes strong inherent spatiotemporal correspondence across visits, removing the need for surface-based post-processing.

[14] Casamitjana A, Sala-Llonch R, Lekadir K, et al. (2025). USLR: An open-source tool for unbiased and smooth longitudinal registration of brain MRI. Medical Image Analysis. doi:10.1016/j.media.2025.103662. PMID: 40592111. Shows: USLR estimates non-linear image trajectories that are smooth across time, unbiased to any timepoint and robust to artefacts, and improves time-consistency in an Alzheimer's disease study.

[15] Magon S, Gaetano L, Chakravarty MM, et al. (2014). White matter lesion filling improves the accuracy of cortical thickness measurements in multiple sclerosis patients: a longitudinal study. BMC Neuroscience. doi:10.1186/1471-2202-15-106. PMID: 25200127. Shows: filling WM lesions with the intensity of neighbouring normal-appearing WM improved the accuracy of cross-sectional and longitudinal cortical thickness measures in 50 MS patients over 3 and 6 years.

[16] Valverde S, Oliver A, Roura E, et al. (2015). Quantifying brain tissue volume in multiple sclerosis with automated lesion segmentation and filling. NeuroImage: Clinical. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2015.10.012. PMID: 26740917. Shows: fully automated lesion segmentation plus filling pipelines introduced acceptable error in GM/WM tissue segmentation compared with expert lesion annotation filling, replacing manual lesion masking.

[17] Pareto D, Sastre-Garriga J, Aymerich FX, et al. (2016). Lesion filling effect in regional brain volume estimations: a study in multiple sclerosis patients with low lesion load. Neuroradiology. doi:10.1007/s00234-016-1654-5. PMID: 26847633. Shows: white matter lesions are erroneously segmented as grey matter, and lesion filling reduced the resulting error in grey matter fraction and regional volumes.

[18] Gao N, Chen H, Guo X, et al. (2024). Geodesic shape regression based deep learning segmentation for assessing longitudinal hippocampal atrophy in dementia progression. NeuroImage: Clinical. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2024.103623. PMID: 38821013. Shows: GeoLongSeg integrates geodesic shape regression into a two-stage network to reduce longitudinal segmentation variability unrelated to disease by enforcing intra-individual morphological consistency.

[19] Tustison NJ, Holbrook AJ, Avants BB, et al. (2019). Longitudinal Mapping of Cortical Thickness Measurements: An Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative-Based Evaluation Study. Journal of Alzheimer's Disease. doi:10.3233/JAD-190283. PMID: 31356207. Shows: an ANTs longitudinal framework that minimises residual variability while maximising between-subject variability gave competitive-to-superior power for detecting longitudinal cortical change versus cross-sectional and longitudinal FreeSurfer.

[20] Beer JC, Tustison NJ, Cook PA, et al. (2020). Longitudinal ComBat: A method for harmonizing longitudinal multi-scanner imaging data. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.117129. PMID: 32640273. Shows: longitudinal ComBat models additive and multiplicative scanner effects in repeated measures and is more powerful for detecting longitudinal change than cross-sectional ComBat, with better type I error control than covariate adjustment.

[21] Richter S, Winzeck S, Correia MM, et al. (2022). Validation of cross-sectional and longitudinal ComBat harmonization methods for magnetic resonance imaging data on a travelling subject cohort. NeuroImage: Reports. doi:10.1016/j.ynirp.2022.100136. PMID: 36507071. Shows: on a travelling-subject cohort, structural metrics showed no significant scanner effect and harmonisation gave no benefit and could obscure true effects, whereas diffusion metrics had marked scanner effects that were successfully harmonised.

[22] Li Q, Pardoe H, Lichter R, et al. (2015). Cortical thickness estimation in longitudinal stroke studies: A comparison of 3 measurement methods. NeuroImage: Clinical. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2014.08.017. PMID: 26110108. Shows: comparing a surface-based (FreeSurfer) and two voxel-based cortical thickness methods in stroke, accuracy of segmentation was worst in peri-lesional cortex, making this the critical region for longitudinal thickness estimates.

[23] Khlif MS, Werden E, Egorova N, et al. (2019). Assessment of longitudinal hippocampal atrophy in the first year after ischemic stroke using automatic segmentation techniques. NeuroImage: Clinical. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2019.102008. PMID: 31711030. Shows: automated hippocampal volumes agreed variably with manual tracing in stroke and lesion presence affected volume estimation; hippocampal volume fell ~3.9 % in first-ever and ~9.2 % in recurrent stroke at three months.

[24] Rosen AFG, Roalf DR, Ruparel K, et al. (2018). Quantitative assessment of structural image quality. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2017.12.059. PMID: 29278774. Shows: quantitative T1 quality measures relate systematically to cortical thickness and, if ignored, bias inference about age-related structural associations, across 1 840 manually rated scans.

[25] Monereo-Sánchez J, de Jong JJA, Drenthen GS, et al. (2021). Quality control strategies for brain MRI segmentation and parcellation: Practical approaches and recommendations - insights from the Maastricht study. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118174. PMID: 34000406. Shows: side-by-side comparison of 12 automated QC strategies against manual editing in 259 subjects gives practical recommendations for reducing measurement error in segmentation-based morphometry.

[26] Puzio T, Matera K, Karwowski J, et al. (2025). Deep learning-based automatic segmentation of brain structures on MRI: A test-retest reproducibility analysis. Computational and Structural Biotechnology Journal. doi:10.1016/j.csbj.2025.04.007. PMID: 40271109. Shows: deep-learning segmentation was highly consistent within the same scanner type but degraded when scanner type/field strength differed, which matters for a mixed-scanner multisite fleet.

[27] Yang Q, Shomal-Zadeh F, Gholipour A (2026). Harmonization in magnetic resonance imaging: A survey of acquisition, image-level, and feature-level methods. Medical Image Analysis. doi:10.1016/j.media.2026.104066. PMID: 41936201. Shows: a survey showing that site/batch effects reduce reproducibility and power and that harmonisation must remove site bias while preserving biological signal, categorising acquisition-, image- and feature-level methods.

[28] Jakimovski D, Zivadinov R, Bergsland N, et al. (2022). Multisite MRI reproducibility of lateral ventricular volume using the NAIMS cooperative pilot dataset. Journal of Neuroimaging. doi:10.1111/jon.12998. PMID: 35384119. Shows: scanner-induced T1-based whole-brain volume error was large relative to clinically meaningful change, and T2-FLAIR-based lateral ventricular volume was proposed as a more reproducible proxy.

[29] Elliott ML, Du J, Nielsen JA, et al. (2026). Precision estimates of longitudinal brain aging capture unexpected individual differences in one year. Nature Communications. doi:10.1038/s41467-026-68886-3. PMID: 41644538. Shows: densely repeated cluster scanning over one year was required to reveal individual differences in brain change that ordinary single scans could not resolve, quantifying measurement error in short intervals.

[30] Schmitz-Koep B, Schultz V, Bongratz F, et al. (2026). Longitudinal assessment of cortical thickness in healthy older individuals: a comparison between CAT12 and freesurfer. Neuroradiology. doi:10.1007/s00234-025-03866-w. PMID: 41384963. Shows: cross-sectional cortical thickness values differed systematically between CAT12 and FreeSurfer, and longitudinal annualised percent change also differed significantly between the two pipelines.

[31] Aamodt EB, Lydersen S, Alnæs D, et al. (2022). Longitudinal Brain Changes After Stroke and the Association With Cognitive Decline. Frontiers in Neurology. doi:10.3389/fneur.2022.856919. PMID: 35720079. Shows: in a multisite stroke cohort followed up to 36 months, cortical thickness and subcortical volumes declined more in the ipsilesional hemisphere and were associated with cognitive decline, demonstrating feasible longitudinal atrophy detection in stroke.

[32] Khan MH, Chakraborty S, Ferris JK, et al. (2026). Estimating white matter hyperintensities volume in individuals with stroke using T1-weighted images. NeuroImage: Reports. doi:10.1016/j.ynirp.2026.100344. PMID: 42011418. Shows: WMH-SynthSeg and SAMSEG can estimate WMH volume from T1 alone in 227 stroke patients, relevant where FLAIR is unavailable, but accuracy varies with the lesion mask used.

BefundLiterature, run twice: where two independent research runs on longitudinal post-stroke volumetry agree and disagreevorschlag2026-10-18Literature run twice (16 topics, 512 refs checked): agreements and six contradictions (mirror filling, raw vs filled, ICV per session, harmonisation, early interval, WMH tool)

Herkunft: 16 literature topics on longitudinal post-stroke volumetry, each researched twice and independently by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub; first run model "clea", second run model "frontier", second run forbidden to open the first); quellen-pruefen 2026-10-18 on the second runs (512 references, 502 with PMID, 502 correct, 0 wrong, 10 without PMID); recommendation-by-recommendation comparison by a Claude Code subagent, checked and committed by Claude Code · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

Running every literature question twice, with a different model and no access to the first answer, is cheap and changes the conclusions in a few decisive places. Agreement across runs marks robust advice; disagreement marks places where the literature does not decide and own data or an own experiment must.

Robust (both runs): a scanner change inside a participant is a directed bias, not noise, and must be modelled with a time-varying scanner term [1]; ICV enters as a covariate or residual, the ratio only as a sensitivity analysis [2,3]; the longitudinal stream is more precise than per-session segmentation for change; QC is a cascade of cheap gates plus a human pass on the flagged tail, and outlier exclusion on global volumes increases unexplained variance [4]; mixed models with random intercept and slope, real time in years, no deletion of incomplete participants.

Contradictions between runs (the literature does not settle these): 1. Mirror (enantiomorphic) filling of infarcts. Run 1 calls it the best-validated classical family; run 2 finds no published volumetric validation at all and recommends against it as a default. Classical WM-intensity filling is strongly mask- and method-dependent: with gold-standard masks FSL lesion_filling left 24.5 % of the lesion area classified as GM versus 0.4 % for LEAP [5]; filling fixes misclassification that masking alone does not [6]. 2. Primary stream raw or filled T1. Split across topics and runs; the two ICV/lesion runs and the inpainting run 2 make the raw stream primary and the filled stream a sensitivity analysis. 3. ICV once per participant or per session. Run 1: baseline, time-invariant. Run 2: session-specific; the ICV estimator itself differs between scanners, segmentation-based TIV is more consistent across scanners [2]. 4. Harmonisation. One run favours longitudinal ComBat over a scanner covariate [7], another the time-varying scanner term in the mixed model [1] and doubts that ComBat removes an effect confounded with time. 5. Early interval (0-6 months). Non-linear fast early atrophy (spline terms) versus "mostly measurement error, fit slopes on later visits". 6. WMH tool. LST-AI is validated on MS lesions only [8]; segcsvdWMH reports the best Dice on heterogeneous ageing/SVD cohorts [9].

Trägt

Second-run references (checked: PMID matches title):

[1] Lee H, Nakamura K, Narayanan S, et al. (2019). Estimating and accounting for the effect of MRI scanner changes on longitudinal whole-brain volume change measurements. NeuroImage 184:555-565. PMID: 30253207. Shows: scanner/sequence changes shift whole-brain change measures with a sign; a fixed corrective term per transition in a mixed model improves fit.

[2] Koike S, Maikusa N, Cai L, et al. (2025). Estimation of the intracranial volume is crucial ... Human Brain Mapping. doi:10.1002/hbm.70405. PMID: 41190774. Shows: eTIV and segmentation-based TIV agree only moderately (ICC 0.78) and sbTIV is more consistent across scanners in longitudinal data.

[3] Brzezinski-Rittner A, Moqadam R, Zeighami Y, Dadar M. (2026). Intracranial volume: To adjust or not to adjust? It is not a matter of if, but how. Imaging Neuroscience 4. doi:10.1162/imag.a.1235. PMID: 42206219. Shows: proportional (ratio) correction can invert associations; covariate and residual adjustment agree with a TIV-matched reference.

[4] Monereo-Sanchez J, de Jong JJA, Drenthen GS, et al. (2021). Quality control strategies for brain MRI segmentation and parcellation. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118174. PMID: 34000406. Shows: Euler number and MRIQC-based exclusion perform best among automated options; excluding outliers on global morphological measures increased relative unexplained variance.

[5] Popescu V, Ran NC, Barkhof F, et al. (2014). Accurate GM atrophy quantification in MS using lesion-filling with co-registered 2D lesion masks. NeuroImage: Clinical. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2014.01.004. PMID: 24567908. Shows: with gold-standard masks LEAP left 0.4 % of the lesion area as GM, FSL lesion_filling 24.5 %.

[6] Battaglini M, Jenkinson M, De Stefano N. (2012). Evaluating and reducing the impact of white matter lesions on brain volume measurements. Human Brain Mapping. doi:10.1002/hbm.21344. PMID: 21882300. Shows: inserted WM lesions caused GM/WM misclassification that persisted when lesions were masked and was removed by filling with normal-appearing WM intensity.

[7] Beer JC, Tustison NJ, Cook PA, et al. (2020). Longitudinal ComBat: a method for harmonizing longitudinal multi-scanner imaging data. NeuroImage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.117129. PMID: 32640273. Shows: longitudinal ComBat was more powerful for longitudinal change and controlled type I error better than cross-sectional ComBat.

[8] Wiltgen T, McGinnis J, Schlaeger S, et al. (2024). LST-AI: a deep learning ensemble for accurate MS lesion segmentation. NeuroImage: Clinical 42. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2024.103611. PMID: 38703470. Shows: three-U-Net ensemble beat LST, SAMSEG and nnU-Net on MS data (Dice 0.65 on MSSEG-1).

[9] Gibson E, Ramirez J, Woods LA, et al. (2024). segcsvdWMH: a CNN-based tool for quantifying white matter hyperintensities in heterogeneous patient cohorts. Human Brain Mapping 45. doi:10.1002/hbm.70104. PMID: 39723488. Shows: mean Dice 0.86 across four heterogeneous datasets, +7.8 % over HyperMapp3r and +21.8 % over SAMSEG.

Own data (2026-10-18, cohort-level, relative values only): within the same site, participants who changed scanner show a global scale step of about +1.5 % in every volume including the ICV proxy, which vanishes when each volume is divided by the same-session ICV; this argues for a per-session ICV in this cohort (contradiction 3).

Trägt nicht

  • No run found a volumetric validation of mirror filling; absence in two searches is not proof of absence.
  • The comparison read the short-answer and recommendation sections, not every paragraph.
  • 10 second-run references carry only a DOI and were not checked by PMID.

Bruchindikator

  • A published validation of enantiomorphic filling for structure volumes, or an own simulation (synthetic lesions in a healthy hemisphere, filled vs original volume per structure) that shows a bias below the smallest detectable change.
  • Own data in which raw and filled streams give the same slopes per structure.

Ablesung

For any methods decision: do it where both runs agree; where they disagree, decide by own data (measured step, test- retest SDC) or by a small own experiment, and record the decision with both positions in the analysis plan.

BefundLiterature: head-size normalisation and handling of structures that overlap a stroke lesionvorschlag2026-10-18Literature (24 refs, checked): head-size normalisation and handling of structures that overlap a stroke lesion

Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) via the compact literature tool, public literature only; quellen-pruefen 2026-10-18 (24 references, 24 with PMID, 24 correct, 0 wrong); checked and committed by Claude Code · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • Estimate head size once per subject and treat it as time-invariant. Longitudinal change must come from tissue, never from a re-estimated ICV: an ICV that itself shrinks with atrophy cancels the very effect being measured [1, 2].
  • ICV estimation is not neutral. FreeSurfer eTIV over-estimated TIV as atrophy progressed in a longitudinal dementia cohort [1], and both SPM- and FreeSurfer-based ICV showed bias related to sex, atrophy or skull size [2]. With 7 sites sharing one protocol, keep the estimation method fixed and QC ICV per subject across visits rather than trusting a single number.
  • SynthSeg is contrast- and resolution-robust and needs no retraining, which is exactly what a multi-scanner MPRAGE cohort needs [3]. In an independent multi-scanner, multi-software comparison (MS) SynthSeg, SAMSEG and FastSurfer gave good to excellent repeatability for large structures, but between-scanner smallest-detectable-change was still 5-29 % - so scanner change, not software, is the dominant error source [4].
  • Do not use the proportional (tissue/ICV) method as the primary normalisation. It flips the sign of the volume-ICV association, changes the direction of sex differences, and is inconsistent with covariate and residual approaches whenever the brain-behaviour association is not null [5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10].
  • Prefer the residual method (regress region on ICV in a reference/normative sample, use the residual) or include ICV/TIV as a covariate in the model. Across 2320 UK Biobank regression models, adjustment and residual agreed with each other but disagreed with crude and proportional estimates [7]; the residual method removed the ICV association with no direction switch and improved single-subject z-score detection (thalamus AUC 0.84 vs 0.79) [10].
  • If ICV/TIV is entered as a covariate, it must be a fixed degree of freedom spent once per subject; adding it as a covariate was the best-performing method in a matched UK Biobank design [8].
  • Lesion filling is about registration and boundary stability, not about inventing volumes. Filling MS/WMH lesions with WM-like intensity reduces grey-matter over-estimation and improves tissue-volume accuracy [11, 12], but naive filling with normal-appearing white matter introduces artefacts whose magnitude scales with total lesion volume (r = 0.84) [13].
  • Mirroring the contralateral hemisphere ("virtual brain grafting/transplantation") is a legitimate way to restore landmarks so that FreeSurfer parcellation of the lesioned hemisphere matches the intact one; it was validated with sulcal-area comparison [15]. But the filled voxels are synthetic, and the accepted use is to enable parcellation/registration, not to read the filled voxel values as biology [13, 15].
  • WMH are a systematic confound for grey-matter volumetry. WMH overlap inflates caudate and other GM volumes; after WMH correction caudate volume declined with age and separated patients, which uncorrected volumes failed to do [14].
  • In our setup, compute SynthSeg volumes on raw T1 and on the lesion-filled T1 and report both, but make the primary longitudinal model the raw-T1 volumes with lesion load as a covariate; use the filled images only where filling demonstrably stabilises the pipeline.
  • Mask, do not trust. Cortical-thickness bias from focal lesions is reduced by fully masking lesion voxels from tissue probability maps rather than only from summarisation [16]; infarcted ipsilesional vertices should be identified from stroke masks and excluded before any cortical-thickness analysis [17].
  • Expect genuine, region-specific, ipsilesional-greater atrophy (thalamus, hippocampus), and treat it as a signal of secondary degeneration, not as a segmentation artefact [18, 19, 20].

Practical recommendation (as written for a multisite longitudinal stroke cohort)

  1. Head size: estimate ICV/TIV once per subject from the baseline (or best-QC) scan with a single fixed method; QC it across visits as a subject-level stability check, and never let a per-visit re-estimation feed the longitudinal model (atrophy-sensitive TIV would cancel the effect [1, 2]).
  2. Normalisation in the LMM: use ICV as a covariate, or model the region on the residual scale, both computed against a fixed reference/normative sample; do not use the proportional fraction as the primary outcome [5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10]. Report the proportional result only as a sensitivity analysis.
  3. Two volume streams: keep the primary longitudinal stream on raw T1 (SynthSeg 2, --robust --qc, per-structure QC >= 0.65, Euler gate) with infarct volume and WMH volume as covariates. Keep the lesion-filled stream as a robustness/secondary stream, reported alongside - and evaluated per structure, because filling effects are structure- and load-dependent [12, 13, 21].
  4. Lesion filling: for the filled stream, prefer a patch-based/in-painting or grafting approach over naive fill-with-NAWM [11, 13]; validate by checking that the difference between raw and filled volumes does not scale with lesion volume (the artefact signature of [13]).
  5. WMH: because WMH overlap inflates subcortical GM volumes and can flip clinical associations [14], always report caudate/putamen/thalamus volumes with and without WMH handling, and consider WMH volume as a covariate rather than only a filling step.
  6. Cortical thickness: exclude/flag lesioned vertices using a priori stroke masks before analysis, and prefer masking lesion voxels out of the tissue probability maps (fully masked) rather than only out of the summarisation [16, 17].
  7. Ipsilesional vs contralesional: do not merge hemispheres. Model ipsilesional and contralesional volumes separately (or their ratio) and expect an ipsilesional excess that is partly secondary degeneration [18, 19, 20, 24]. Where a structure directly overlaps the lesion, flag/exclude it per subject and structure, and record that in the QC table.
  8. Scanner/harmonisation: cross-scanner smallest detectable change dominates software differences [4], so keep longComBat in the plan, harmonise within scanner/site, and report the lesion-filled/raw sensitivity in the same framework.
  9. Reporting: give head-size-normalised volumes with the adjustment method named explicitly, since "ICV-corrected" is not one method and different methods disagree [7, 9, 22].

Trägt

24 references, each retrieved in the task with the literature tool and checked (PMID matches title, DOI registered at doi.org). Each reference carries one "Shows:" sentence from its abstract.

[1] Pengas G, Pereira JM, Williams GB, et al., 2009, Comparative reliability of total intracranial volume estimation methods and the influence of atrophy in a longitudinal semantic dementia cohort, J Neuroimaging, doi:10.1111/j.1552-6569.2008.00246.x, PMID:18494772. Shows: in a longitudinally imaged semantic-dementia cohort, fully automated FreeSurfer over-estimated TIV as atrophy progressed while a mid-cranial PD-based method was least susceptible, so TIV estimation is not atrophy-invariant - the reason to fix head size per subject in a longitudinal study.

[2] Nordenskjöld R, Malmberg F, Larsson EM, et al., 2013, Intracranial volume estimated with commonly used methods could introduce bias in studies including brain volume measurements, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2013.06.068, PMID:23827332. Shows: comparing SPM and FreeSurfer ICV with reference segmentations in 399 scans of 75-year-olds found both over-estimated ICV, SPM biased by sex and atrophy and FreeSurfer by skull size, and hippocampal normalisation associations changed with the ICV measure - direct evidence that ICV choice biases results.

[3] Billot B, Greve DN, Puonti O, et al., 2023, SynthSeg: Segmentation of brain MRI scans of any contrast and resolution without retraining, Med Image Anal, doi:10.1016/j.media.2023.102789, PMID:36857946. Shows: SynthSeg, trained on synthetic scans with fully randomised contrast and resolution, segments scans across six modalities and ten resolutions without retraining, which is what justifies one model across several scanner models and visits.

[4] van Nederpelt DR, Amiri H, Brouwer I, et al., 2023, Reliability of brain atrophy measurements in multiple sclerosis using MRI: an assessment of six freely available software packages for cross-sectional analyses, Neuroradiology, doi:10.1007/s00234-023-03189-8, PMID:37526657. Shows: across three 3 T scanners and six packages (including SAMSEG, FastSurfer, SynthSeg), large-structure ICC was good to excellent and filled vs non-filled images did not differ clearly, but between-scanner smallest detectable change was 4.83-29.25 %, identifying scanner change as the dominant error source.

[5] Voevodskaya O, Simmons A, Nordenskjöld R, et al., 2014, The effects of intracranial volume adjustment approaches on multiple regional MRI volumes in healthy aging and Alzheimer's disease, Front Aging Neurosci, doi:10.3389/fnagi.2014.00264, PMID:25339897. Shows: raw regional volumes correlate positively with ICV while volume-to-ICV fractions correlate negatively, and only residual correction eliminated the ICV correlation and the sex differences - the core argument against the proportional method.

[6] Sanfilipo MP, Benedict RH, Zivadinov R, et al., 2004, Correction for intracranial volume in analysis of whole brain atrophy in multiple sclerosis: the proportion vs. residual method, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2004.03.037, PMID:15275929. Shows: injecting systematic and random ICV/parenchymal error showed the proportion method shifted normalized means and reduced power under random error, whereas residual-based predicted volumes were largely unaffected and stable in power - early evidence preferring the residual method.

[7] Wang J, Hill-Jarrett T, Buto P, et al., 2024, Comparison of approaches to control for intracranial volume in research on the association of brain volumes with cognitive outcomes, Hum Brain Mapp, doi:10.1002/hbm.26633, PMID:38433682. Shows: across 2320 UK Biobank regression models (N = 41,964), the covariate and residual approaches agreed but disagreed with the crude and proportional approaches, and for the hippocampus the proportional approach even reversed the sign of the cognition association - so "ICV-corrected" is not one method.

[8] Dhamala E, Ooi LQR, Chen J, et al., 2022, Proportional intracranial volume correction differentially biases behavioral predictions across neuroanatomical features, sexes, and development, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2022.119485, PMID:35843514. Shows: proportional ICV correction reduced prediction accuracy from surface area and GM volume but increased it from cortical thickness, and changed cross-sex/cross-age generalisability, demonstrating that the choice of ICV correction has feature- and subgroup-specific effects.

[9] Hu M, Lou Y, Zhu C, et al., 2024, Evaluating the Impact of Intracranial Volume Correction Approaches on the Quantification of Intracranial Structures in MRI: A Systematic Analysis, J Magn Reson Imaging, doi:10.1002/jmri.28974, PMID:37702125. Shows: against a TIV-matched gold standard in 141 volunteers, the residual method had the highest consistency (94.4 %), while unadjusted structures differed significantly between sexes - supporting residual correction.

[10] Opfer R, Krüger J, Spies L, et al., 2022, Single-subject analysis of regional brain volumetric measures can be strongly influenced by the method for head size adjustment, Neuroradiology, doi:10.1007/s00234-022-02961-6, PMID:35462574. Shows: in 5059 normative scans the residual method removed the TIV association and cut between-subject variability more than the proportional method, and gave a higher AUC for detecting thalamic atrophy in MS (0.84 vs 0.79) - the single-subject argument for the residual method.

[11] Prados F, Cardoso MJ, Kanber B, et al., 2016, A multi-time-point modality-agnostic patch-based method for lesion filling in multiple sclerosis, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2016.06.053, PMID:27377222. Shows: a 5D patch-based non-local-means lesion filling that fills lesions with the most plausible texture introduced fewer artefacts and spurious edges than standard fill-with-nearby-WM methods, notably where lesions abut ventricles or deep grey matter.

[12] Valverde S, Oliver A, Lladó X, 2014, A white matter lesion-filling approach to improve brain tissue volume measurements, Neuroimage Clin, doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2014.08.016, PMID:25379419. Shows: refilling WM lesions with intensities drawn from the slice WM distribution reduced the tissue-volume deviation from the lesion-free ground truth across 1.5 T and 3 T data and two segmentation tools, i.e. lesion filling improves tissue-volume accuracy.

[13] van der Weijden CWJ, Pitombeira MS, Haveman YRA, et al., 2022, The effect of lesion filling on brain network analysis in multiple sclerosis using structural magnetic resonance imaging, Insights Imaging, doi:10.1186/s13244-022-01198-4, PMID:35347460. Shows: artefacts in lesion-filled T1w images correlated with total lesion volume (r = 0.84, p < 0.001) and had a major impact on GM segmentation accuracy, so lesion filling should be applied cautiously at higher lesion loads - the load-dependence warning for our filling step.

[14] Dadar M, Potvin O, Camicioli R, et al., 2021, Beware of white matter hyperintensities causing systematic errors in FreeSurfer gray matter segmentations!, Hum Brain Mapp, doi:10.1002/hbm.25398, PMID:33783933. Shows: in 1077 ADNI participants (4321 time-points) WMH overlapped more with GM structures at higher WMH burden, and without WMH correction caudate volume increased with age and failed to separate AD, which changed to decline and group separation after correction - WMH handling flips clinical associations.

[15] Solodkin A, Hasson U, Siugzdaite R, et al., 2010, Virtual brain transplantation (VBT): a method for accurate image registration and parcellation in large cortical stroke, Arch Ital Biol, PMID:21175010. Shows: filling a large left-hemisphere stroke by transplanting tissue mirrored from the non-stroke hemisphere produced no FreeSurfer parcellation difference for 12 regions between lesioned and non-lesioned hemispheres - validation that mirroring restores landmarks for parcellation.

[16] Brennan D, Schneider ALC, Shinohara RT, et al., 2026, Contusions bias cortical thickness estimates after traumatic brain injury: A TRACK-TBI study, Neuroimage Clin, doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2026.104003, PMID:42114207. Shows: in 86 TRACK-TBI participants, unmasked and summarisation-only masking left lesion-related cross-sectional and longitudinal cortical-thickness bias, while fully masking lesion voxels from tissue probability maps attenuated the cross-sectional bias - a concrete lesion-handling recommendation.

[17] Khlif MS, Egorova-Brumley N, Bird LJ, et al., 2022, Cortical thinning 3 years after ischaemic stroke is associated with cognitive impairment and APOE ε4, Neuroimage Clin, doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2022.103200, PMID:36116165. Shows: in the CANVAS stroke cohort, infarcted ipsilesional vertices were identified from a-priori stroke lesion masks and excluded before mixed-effects cortical-thickness analysis - a concrete example of excluding lesioned cortex in a longitudinal stroke study.

[18] Yassi N, Malpas CB, Campbell BC, et al., 2015, Contralesional thalamic surface atrophy and functional disconnection 3 months after ischemic stroke, Cerebrovasc Dis, doi:10.1159/000381105, PMID:25823493. Shows: in 15 anterior-circulation stroke patients imaged up to 3 months, contralesional thalamic volume fell significantly (-2.1 %, p = 0.047) with declining functional connectivity and atrophy correlated with stroke severity - remote structural and functional change coexist.

[19] Geng J, Gao F, Ramirez J, et al., 2023, Secondary thalamic atrophy related to brain infarction may contribute to post-stroke cognitive impairment, J Stroke Cerebrovasc Dis, doi:10.1016/j.jstrokecerebrovasdis.2022.106895, PMID:36495644. Shows: after controlling for head size and global brain atrophy, infarct volume independently correlated with ipsilateral thalamic volume (beta = -0.069, p = 0.024), and left thalamic atrophy correlated with poorer cognition - the template for a head-size- and atrophy-adjusted remote-atrophy analysis.

[20] Aamodt EB, Lydersen S, Alnæs D, et al., 2022, Longitudinal Brain Changes After Stroke and the Association With Cognitive Decline, Front Neurol, doi:10.3389/fneur.2022.856919, PMID:35720079. Shows: in 244 Nor-COAST survivors with FreeSurfer at baseline/18/36 months, cortices and subcortices showed greater ipsi- than contralesional atrophy rate (more prominent right), with medial-temporal atrophy after left strokes and larger lesions after right strokes linked to cognitive decline.

[21] Guo C, Ferreira D, Fink K, et al., 2019, Repeatability and reproducibility of FreeSurfer, FSL-SIENAX and SPM brain volumetric measurements and the effect of lesion filling in multiple sclerosis, Eur Radiol, doi:10.1007/s00330-018-5710-x, PMID:30242503. Shows: repeat scanning of MS patients on three scanners showed within-scanner variability lower than between-scanner, ICV normalisation improved FreeSurfer and SPM reproducibility, and MS lesion filling changed FSL/SPM output but not FreeSurfer - directly relevant to a multi-scanner ICV-normalised volumetry.

[22] Brzezinski-Rittner A, Moqadam R, Zeighami Y, et al., 2026, Intracranial volume: To adjust or not to adjust? It is not a matter of if, but how, Imaging Neurosci (Camb), doi:10.1162/IMAG.a.1235, PMID:42206219. Shows: against a TIV-matched UK Biobank gold standard, adding TIV as a covariate was the best-performing adjustment method, and smoothing of cortical metrics itself biased sex-difference estimates - the most recent head-to-head of ICV adjustment methods.

[23] Radwan AM, Emsell L, Blommaert J, et al., 2021, Virtual brain grafting: Enabling whole brain parcellation in the presence of large lesions, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.117731, PMID:33454411. Shows: Virtual Brain Grafting generates a lesion-free T1 so that recon-all parcellation succeeds in heterogeneous focal pathology, with mean Dice 0.894 (unilateral) / 0.903 (bilateral) versus 0.617 without grafting, and quantifies the influence of lesion volume and overlap on labeling accuracy.

[24] Zavaliangos-Petropulu A, Lo B, Donnelly MR, et al., 2022, Chronic Stroke Sensorimotor Impairment Is Related to Smaller Hippocampal Volumes: An ENIGMA Analysis, J Am Heart Assoc, doi:10.1161/JAHA.121.025109, PMID:35574963. Shows: pooling 357 chronic stroke patients across 18 ENIGMA cohorts, sensorimotor impairment was associated with ipsilesional (p = 0.005) but not contralesional hippocampal volume, and hippocampal volume was associated with lesion size - evidence that even non-lesioned, ipsilesional structures carry secondary-degeneration signal and should be analysed per hemisphere.

Trägt nicht

Open questions from the report:

  • Which ICV estimator is most atrophy-stable for a mixed Siemens MPRAGE cohort (eTIV vs SynthSeg-derived vs SAMSEG-derived), given that all automated estimators showed some atrophy- or skull-related bias in the older literature [1, 2]? A within-cohort repeat/scan-rescan check on the same subjects would answer this.
  • Does mirror-based contour filling (our infarct-filling step) preserve parcellation accuracy at the lesion margin specifically, or only globally? The grafting literature [15, 23] reports region-level agreement, but the load-dependent artefact warning [13] has not been tested for mirroring in stroke (mostly tested in MS/glioma).
  • How large is the residual-vs-covariate difference in a stroke cohort with WMH and atrophy, and does the choice interact with longComBat?
  • Best criterion to flag a structure as "lesion-overlapping vs secondary-degenerated": overlap fraction, distance from infarct, or ipsilesional-contralesional asymmetry?
  • Whether WMH filling with normal-appearing WM intensity reproduces the caudate-volume bias of [14] in our pipeline, or whether the WMH-filling step already mitigates it.

Claims rest on abstracts, not full texts, unless the report says otherwise.

Bruchindikator

A full-text reading or a later consensus paper contradicts a cited effect size or recommendation; or our own cohort data show the opposite (for example no scanner effect at a scanner change).

Ablesung

Report written 2026-10-18 by Clara (hospital model hub), question set by Claude Code after a pipeline audit; sources verified with quellen-pruefen the same day.

BefundLiterature: statistics for longitudinal brain volumes after stroke in a multi-site cohortvorschlag2026-10-18Literature (27 refs, checked): mixed models with random slopes, non-linear early atrophy, MAR-aware missing data, longComBat over site covariate, scanner change within site biases 2-4 %

Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) via the compact literature tool, checked by Claude Code 2026-10-18 (quellen-pruefen 0 wrong, 'Shows' spot checks against abstracts) · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • Use a linear mixed-effects model with subject-specific random intercepts and random slopes for time; this is the standard for longitudinal neuroimaging and is markedly more powerful than repeated-measures ANOVA or per-subject slope extraction [1].
  • Code time as continuous years since baseline for the atrophy rate, and add a non-linear term (spline or piecewise segments) because post-stroke atrophy is not linear — the first ~3 months are 2-4x faster than later periods [5][6].
  • Missing visits: do not use listwise deletion. Mixed models already give valid inference under MAR; where the missingness is informative, use FIML / multiple imputation / IPW [3]. Expect heavy dropout: attrition to <50% by 36 months is described in comparable stroke cohorts [7].
  • Site and scanner: include site and scanner as covariates in the model as the minimal option, but this does not remove scanner-related variance — site explained >50% of volume variance even with a harmonised protocol [13]. Use longitudinal ComBat for longitudinal data; it is more powerful for detecting change than cross-sectional ComBat and controls type I error better than "scanner as covariate" [11][10][12].
  • Scanner change within a site is a real, measurable bias (significant differences in >=67% of cortical/subcortical regions, ~2-4%, after a 3T upgrade [14]); correct it with a transfer term estimated in controls (or in travelling subjects) and model it as a fixed effect [14][15].
  • Head size: do not divide by ICV. Proportional correction biases results and can reverse the direction of associations; residual/adjusted ICV (or ICV as a covariate) is preferable [16][17].
  • Lesion volume must be a covariate: it is an independent predictor of post-stroke cognition and larger lesions are associated with greater later atrophy [4][8][9].
  • Measurement error sets the floor: longitudinal FreeSurfer improves precision by 15-50% over cross-sectional [18][19]; with 2-year follow-up reliability of individual change is only modest and 2-year designs need ~2.7x more subjects than 4-year designs [19]. Report a smallest detectable change, not just a p-value.
  • Normative modelling (centile/z-deviation from a healthy reference) adds individual-level deviation on top of the group-level LME and captures heterogeneity that case-control averages away [20][21].
  • Power: with ~600 patients, 4 visits and long follow-up you are in a comfortable range for whole-brain and subcortical change, but individual-level claims about small structures (entorhinal, choroid plexus) remain underpowered [18][19][2].

Practical recommendation (as written for our setup)

Model (per structure, per hemisphere where ipsi/contralesional contrast is of interest):

  • Outcome: harmonised regional volume (longitudinal FreeSurfer stream, [18][19]), not divided by ICV.
  • Fixed effects: time in years since stroke (continuous) + a non-linear term (piecewise with break at ~3-6 months, or natural spline); time x group interaction of interest; ICV [16][17]; age, sex; chronic lesion volume [7][8]; WMH volume [8]; scanner identity as a factor [14][15]; cognitive status at 6 months if it is the exposure of interest [6].
  • Random effects: random intercept + random slope for time at the subject level; site as a random effect (used in [8]); scanner as a random effect is an alternative but see the warning in [13] that variance-component modelling explained site effects poorly.
  • Harmonisation: longitudinal ComBat with site/scanner as batch and biological covariates preserved [11]; report the unharmonised model as a sensitivity analysis. Use cross-sectional ComBat only if you cannot use the longitudinal variant [10][12].
  • Missing data: REML is the default (valid under MAR); add MICE/FIML/imputation for missing covariates and pre-specified auxiliary variables [3][4]. Report the visit-specific n and the dropout pattern.
  • Scanner changes: maintain a scanner/metadata log; if a change occurred, acquire a bridging sample and estimate a transfer/corrective term from controls as in [14]; re-run the model with a scanner-change indicator.
  • Reporting: for every structure, publish the within-subject SD/CV and smallest detectable change from a test-retest or literature source [20][21][18]; report the group-level atrophy rate with a CI and, separately, the fraction of individuals whose change exceeds the smallest detectable change.
  • Individual-level layer: add a normative-model z-score at baseline and its change as a secondary outcome [22][23], with explicit acknowledgement of the sample-size/uncertainty limits of normative models [24].
  • Power: run the power analysis for the exact LME with the planned visit-specific n and the observed within-subject correlation, and report sensitivity over a range of assumptions [2].

Trägt

Every reference retrieved in the task; quellen-pruefen: 0 wrong PMIDs/titles, DOIs registered (2026-10-18); one "Shows" sentence per reference from its abstract; spot checks consistent.

Trägt nicht

Abstract-level evidence, a literature map rather than a systematic review. Open questions:

  • Which scanner/site effects remain after longitudinal ComBat in our cohort, given that site effects persisted even under a harmonised protocol in [13]? Only measurable with a repeat-scan bridging sample.
  • To what extent is our dropout informative (MNAR) with respect to the volume trajectory, and are IPW or a joint model needed in addition to REML? [3][4] give the methods, not the answer for this cohort.
  • Is a single non-linear time model sufficient, or should the early (<6 months) and late (>36 months) phases be analysed as separate models? [5] motivates the question but does not settle the model choice.
  • How large is the scanner-change correction term for our specific upgrade, and can controls provide it? [14] gives a 3.4% figure for a 1.5T->3T change; this is not transferable without our own bridge data.
  • Does ICV itself drift after a scanner change or after large infarcts, and how should it then be handled [13][17]?
  • Which normative reference model matches our segmentation version and age range, and is its uncertainty acceptable for individual-level claims at ~600 patients [23][24]?
  • Thalamic atrophy specifically: [5][7] show stroke-driven thalamic loss, but the relative contribution of direct thalamic infarction vs secondary degeneration to a "thalamic atrophy rate" is not resolved by the retrieved papers.

Bruchindikator

A cited abstract that does not contain the claim, or a newer study contradicting the recommendation.

Ablesung

[1] Bernal-Rusiel JL, Reuter M, Greve DN, et al., 2013, Spatiotemporal linear mixed effects modeling for the mass-univariate analysis of longitudinal neuroimage data, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2013.05.049, PMID:23702413. Shows: extends linear mixed-effects modelling to mass-univariate longitudinal neuroimaging and reports a dramatic gain in statistical power and repeatability of findings over two alternative methods while controlling the false-positive rate — the methodological basis for the recommended LME.

[2] Harrall KK, Muller KE, Starling AP, et al., 2023, Power and sample size analysis for longitudinal mixed models of health in populations exposed to environmental contaminants: a tutorial, BMC Med Res Methodol, doi:10.1186/s12874-022-01819-y, PMID:36635621. Shows: a practical tutorial showing how to align a power analysis with the planned longitudinal mixed model, which inputs are needed (effect size, SD, within-subject correlation) and how to report sensitivity analyses — the basis for the power recommendation.

[3] Li L, Bayat M, Hayes TB, et al., 2025, Missing data approaches for longitudinal neuroimaging research: Examples from the Adolescent Brain and Cognitive Development (ABCD) Study, Dev Cogn Neurosci, doi:10.1016/j.dcn.2025.101563, PMID:40413902. Shows: listwise deletion is not recommended for longitudinal neuroimaging because it can bias results substantially; multiple imputation, propensity-score weighting and full information maximum likelihood are advocated — the basis for the missing-data recommendation.

[4] Chandrasekaran G, Xie SX, 2024, Improving Regression Analysis with Imputation in a Longitudinal Study of Alzheimer's Disease, J Alzheimers Dis, doi:10.3233/JAD-231047, PMID:38640151. Shows: in 1063 ADNI subjects with mild cognitive impairment, MICE imputation before a linear mixed-effects model gave valid but tighter confidence intervals, i.e. improved efficiency over analysis without imputation, while warning that an invalid imputation approach can compromise the analysis.

[5] Brodtmann A, Khlif MS, Egorova N, et al., 2020, Dynamic Regional Brain Atrophy Rates in the First Year After Ischemic Stroke, Stroke, doi:10.1161/STROKEAHA.120.030256, PMID:32772680. Shows: with a linear mixed-effect model over baseline/3/12 months in 113 stroke and 40 control participants, ipsilesional hemispheric, hippocampal and thalamic atrophy rates were 2-4 times greater in the first 3 months than between 3 and 12 months — evidence that time must be modelled non-linearly.

[6] Brodtmann A, Werden E, Khlif MS, et al., 2021, Neurodegeneration Over 3 Years Following Ischaemic Stroke: Findings From the Cognition and Neocortical Volume After Stroke Study, Front Neurol, doi:10.3389/fneur.2021.754204, PMID:34744989. Shows: 3-month-to-3-year total brain volume loss was 20.3 cm3 (SD 14.8) in 93 stroke survivors versus 14.2 cm3 (SD 13.2) in 39 controls (adjusted difference 7.88 cm3, 95% CI 2.84-12.91, p = 0.002), and was larger in those cognitively impaired at 3 months (adjusted difference 10.42 cm3, 95% CI 3.04-17.80) — a concrete post-stroke atrophy rate and its link to early cognitive impairment.

[7] Aamodt EB, Lydersen S, Alnæs D, et al., 2022, Longitudinal Brain Changes After Stroke and the Association With Cognitive Decline, Front Neurol, doi:10.3389/fneur.2022.856919, PMID:35720079. Shows: in 244 stroke survivors from five hospitals followed to 36 months (145 scanned at 18 months, 72 at 36 months), atrophy was greater ipsilesionally and more so on the right, and greater atrophy and larger stroke lesion volumes were associated with cognitive decline — evidence for lesion volume as covariate and for the attrition pattern.

[8] de Kort FAS, Coenen M, Weaver NA, et al., 2023, White Matter Hyperintensity Volume and Poststroke Cognition: An Individual Patient Data Pooled Analysis of 9 Ischemic Stroke Cohort Studies, Stroke, doi:10.1161/STROKEAHA.123.044297, PMID:37901947. Shows: in 1568 pooled patients, linear mixed models with study site as a random effect found a dose-dependent inverse association of WMH volume with all four cognitive domains independent of acute infarct volume, lacunes and old infarcts — direct precedent for site as a random effect and for lesion/WMH covariates.

[9] Ball EL, Shah M, Ross E, et al., 2023, Predictors of post-stroke cognitive impairment using acute structural MRI neuroimaging: A systematic review and meta-analysis, Int J Stroke, doi:10.1177/17474930221120349, PMID:35924821. Shows: pooling 26 papers (27 populations, N = 13,114) with MRI at stroke onset and cognitive outcome at least 3 months later, cerebral atrophy (OR 2.48, 95% CI 1.15-4.62), microbleeds (OR 1.36, 1.08-1.70) and increasing WMH severity (OR 1.26, 1.06-1.49) were associated with later cognitive outcome.

[10] Fortin JP, Cullen N, Sheline YI, et al., 2018, Harmonization of cortical thickness measurements across scanners and sites, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2017.11.024, PMID:29155184. Shows: across 11 scanners in two multi-site studies ComBat removed unwanted scanner variability while increasing the power and reproducibility of subsequent analyses, and is applicable to lifespan trajectories — the core cross-sectional harmonisation reference.

[11] Beer JC, Tustison NJ, Cook PA, et al., 2020, Longitudinal ComBat: A method for harmonizing longitudinal multi-scanner imaging data, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.117129, PMID:32640273. Shows: with 663 ADNI participants, longitudinal ComBat was more powerful for detecting longitudinal change than cross-sectional ComBat and controlled the type I error rate better than unharmonised data with scanner as a covariate, while modelling additive and multiplicative scanner effects — the key harmonisation reference for a longitudinal design.

[12] Radua J, Vieta E, Shinohara R, et al., 2020, Increased power by harmonizing structural MRI site differences with the ComBat batch adjustment method in ENIGMA, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.116956, PMID:32470572. Shows: in 33 sites (~6000 subjects) ComBat substantially increased statistical significance versus random-effects meta-analysis and slightly increased it versus mixed-effects mega-analysis, with R functions robust to partially missing regions and transferable between datasets — evidence for the power gain and for practical applicability.

[13] Clark KA, O'Donnell CM, Elliott MA, et al., 2023, Intersite brain MRI volumetric biases persist even in a harmonized multisubject study of multiple sclerosis, J Neuroimaging, doi:10.1111/jon.13147, PMID:37587544. Shows: with scan/rescan data from 11 volunteers at four sites, site explained >50% (expanding to >75% across two vendors) of the variation in most estimated volumes, random-effect model assumptions were violated for most comparisons, and differences persisted despite protocol harmonisation — the cautionary counterweight to pure covariate/variance-component adjustment.

[14] Sinnecker T, Schädelin S, Benkert P, et al., 2022, Brain atrophy measurement over a MRI scanner change in multiple sclerosis, Neuroimage Clin, doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2022.103148, PMID:36007437. Shows: after a 1.5T-to-3T scanner upgrade the mean whole-brain volume change was -4.1% in patients and -3.4% in controls; a fixed corrective term of 3.4% (95% CI 3.13-3.67) estimated from controls removed the association of atrophy measures with scanner type (p = 0.29) in a linear mixed-effects model — the template for handling a scanner change with bridge data.

[15] Medawar E, Thieleking R, Manuilova I, et al., 2021, Estimating the effect of a scanner upgrade on measures of grey matter structure for longitudinal designs, PLoS One, doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0239021, PMID:34610020. Shows: comparing 3T Verio and Skyra in 116 healthy adults using the longitudinal FreeSurfer stream, between-scanner ICC was >0.8 but paired tests showed significant differences in at least 67% of regions with 2-4% differences, only slightly reduced by offline gradient-distortion correction, plus systematic differences in contrast-based quality metrics.

[16] Dhamala E, Ooi LQR, Chen J, et al., 2022, Proportional intracranial volume correction differentially biases behavioral predictions across neuroanatomical features, sexes, and development, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2022.119485, PMID:35843514. Shows: proportional ICV correction reduced predictive accuracy for surface area and grey matter volume while increasing it for cortical thickness in 1013 adults and 1823 children, and changed the generalisability of models across sexes and age groups — evidence against treating proportional ICV correction as neutral.

[17] Opfer R, Krüger J, Spies L, et al., 2022, Single-subject analysis of regional brain volumetric measures can be strongly influenced by the method for head size adjustment, Neuroradiology, doi:10.1007/s00234-022-02961-6, PMID:35462574. Shows: in a normative database of 5059 T1w images and 127 MS patients, the residual TIV method removed the TIV association in all regions and reduced between-subject variability more than the proportion method, and gave a larger AUC for thalamic atrophy (0.84 vs 0.79); method differences exceeded one z-score point in 5% of subjects.

[18] Reuter M, Schmansky NJ, Rosas HD, et al., 2012, Within-subject template estimation for unbiased longitudinal image analysis, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2012.02.084, PMID:22430496. Shows: unbiased within-subject template creation for longitudinal FreeSurfer processing reduces variability and avoids over-regularisation, giving a significant increase in precision and discrimination power that can allow smaller sample sizes or shorter trials — the reason to use the longitudinal processing stream.

[19] Jovicich J, Marizzoni M, Sala-Llonch R, et al., 2013, Brain morphometry reproducibility in multi-center 3T MRI studies: a comparison of cross-sectional and longitudinal segmentations, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2013.05.007, PMID:23668971. Shows: across-session test-retest data from 8 sites in 4 countries and three vendors showed longitudinal FreeSurfer segmentation is systematically more reliable across sites regardless of MRI system differences, with reproducibility errors about half of those of cross-sectional analysis for volume segmentations.

[20] Rusinek H, Bokacheva L, Chen H, et al., 2026, Test-retest reliability of FreeSurfer measures of neurodegeneration, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2026.121920, PMID:41966233. Shows: two 3T scans ~5.5 weeks apart in 100 older adults showed longitudinal processing improved precision by 15-50% (largest gain for entorhinal thickness), large well-defined regions (hippocampus, AD signature) were more reliable than small complex ones (entorhinal cortex, choroid plexus), and image quality (Euler characteristic) was the only factor significantly associated with variability.

[21] Vidal-Piñeiro D, Sørensen Ø, Strømstad M, et al., 2025, Reliability of structural brain change in cognitively healthy adult samples, Imaging Neurosci (Camb), doi:10.1162/imag_a_00547, PMID:40800869. Shows: reliability of individual change is modest at 2-year follow-up; longer follow-up increases reliability substantially while adding observations helps little; 2-year designs need about 2.7x (4-year) and 4.0x (6-year) more individuals for comparable power; subcortical volume is more reliable than cortical measures and the longitudinal FreeSurfer stream clearly outperforms the cross-sectional one.

[22] Marquand AF, Rezek I, Buitelaar J, et al., 2016, Understanding Heterogeneity in Clinical Cohorts Using Normative Models: Beyond Case-Control Studies, Biol Psychiatry, doi:10.1016/j.biopsych.2015.12.023, PMID:26927419. Shows: introduces normative modelling to parse heterogeneity in clinical cohorts with individual-level predictions, identifying outliers within a healthy reference distribution and relating the magnitude of deviation to symptomatology — the conceptual basis for adding individual deviation analysis.

[23] Ge R, Yu Y, Qi YX, et al., 2024, Normative modelling of brain morphometry across the lifespan with CentileBrain: algorithm benchmarking and model optimisation, Lancet Digit Health, doi:10.1016/S2589-7500(23)00250-9, PMID:38395541. Shows: benchmarking across 37,407 healthy individuals from 87 datasets showed multivariate fractional polynomial regression with non-linear age terms and linear global-measure covariates to be optimal, accurate across the lifespan and stable longitudinally over 2 years, with performance plateauing beyond about 3000 participants.

[24] Bozek J, Griffanti L, Lau S, et al., 2023, Normative models for neuroimaging markers: Impact of model selection, sample size and evaluation criteria, Neuroimage, doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2023.119864, PMID:36621581. Shows: simulation of normative models for hippocampal volume across sample sizes from 50 to 50,000 shows that bias decreases only modestly with sample size, that uncertainty of model performance is substantial even at large neuroimaging sample sizes and rises sharply at the ends of the age range, and that flexible models perform better — a caution for individual-level normative claims.

[25] Khlif MS, Werden E, Bird LJ, et al., 2022, Atrophy of Ipsilesional Hippocampal Subfields Vary Over First Year After Ischemic Stroke, J Magn Reson Imaging, doi:10.1002/jmri.28009, PMID:34837426. Shows: in 92 stroke and 39 healthy participants with FreeSurfer subfield segmentation, there was no group difference at 3 months but a significant difference in ipsilesional whole-hippocampal volume reduction between 3 and 12 months (p = 0.027, ipsilesional volume smaller at 12 months p = 0.035), with 1.19% ipsilesional atrophy over 9 months, the hippocampal tail contributing 22.7% and CA1 reduction significant ipsilesionally (p = 0.007) and discriminating better than whole hippocampus (p < 0.01).

[26] Pendlebury ST, Rothwell PM, 2009, Prevalence, incidence, and factors associated with pre-stroke and post-stroke dementia: a systematic review and meta-analysis, Lancet Neurol, doi:10.1016/S1474-4422(09)70236-4, PMID:19782001. Shows: pooling 30 cohorts (7511 patients), pre-stroke dementia prevalence was 9-14%; post-stroke (<1 year) dementia ranged from 7.4% (population-based first-ever stroke excluding pre-stroke dementia) to 41.3% (hospital-based recurrent stroke including it), with cumulative incidence beyond the first year only about 3.0% per year; medial temporal lobe atrophy was strongly associated with pre-stroke dementia.

[27] Manco C, Cortese R, Leoncini M, et al., 2024, Hippocampal atrophy and white matter lesions characteristics can predict evolution to dementia in patients with vascular mild cognitive impairment, J Neurol Sci, doi:10.1016/j.jns.2024.123163, PMID:39128160. Shows: in 110 patients with vascular MCI followed for 2 years, 32 progressed to vascular dementia; converters had lower hippocampal volume (p = 0.015) and higher lesion volumes in specific tracts, hippocampal volume alone did not fully explain progression (p = 0.059) but combined with WMH volume the model was significant (p = 0.035).

BefundLiterature: methods and reporting checklist for a multisite longitudinal structural MRI cohortvorschlag2026-10-18Literature (22 refs, checked): methods and reporting checklist for a multisite longitudinal structural MRI cohort

Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) via the compact literature tool, public literature only; quellen-pruefen 2026-10-18 (22 references, 22 with PMID, 22 correct, 0 wrong); checked and committed by Claude Code · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • The binding skeleton for our paper is STROBE (cohort checklist, 22 items) for the epidemiological/design part plus COBIDAS/OHBM for the imaging/analysis part; STROBE governs participants, flow, missing data, descriptive data, outcome definitions and generalisability [5, 6], COBIDAS governs acquisition, preprocessing, model specification and code/data sharing [1, 2].
  • Empirical checks show that reporting is the weak link, not the analysis: across published studies methods and results reporting "can differ greatly" and clarifies far less than the guidelines intend [3], and the checklist literature itself identifies transparency/reproducibility as a persisting obstacle that checklists have not yet solved [4].
  • What is most often missing in practice is not the headline model but the audit trail: exact software versions and container/singularity images, the full preprocessing parameter set, QC metric + threshold + number of excluded scans, and an a-priori analysis plan deposited before unblinding [1, 3, 4, 7].
  • Pre-registration / a dated analysis plan is the single cheapest protection against analytical flexibility: 70 teams analysing one dataset with the same 9 hypotheses chose no two identical workflows and reached materially different results [22]; pre-registration formalises the prediction/postdiction distinction [7].
  • For multisite data, scanner variance must be quantified and reported, because it exceeded age- and sex-related variance for almost all structural features in a large multisite cohort [18]; ComBat removes it and increases power and reproducibility when applied correctly [14, 15].
  • Harmonisation is not a solved problem for exactly our design: no method (there including ComBat and LongComBat) successfully harmonised a longitudinal dataset with scanner change within subject [17]; longComBat is validated for cross-sectional and longitudinal use on travelling subjects [16], so our longitudinal ComBat claims need an explicit within-subject scanner-change sensitivity analysis.
  • QC must be reported as a quantitative, pre-specified gate and then handled in the model: the Euler number tracks manual quality ratings and detects "unusable" images with AUC 0.98-0.99 [11], automated QC generalises to unseen sites only at ~76% accuracy and remains susceptible to site effects [13], and thresholding by QC score attenuates but does not eliminate the bias quality induces on cortical estimates [12].
  • Where rating or visual reading is involved, blinding/calibration and agreement statistics belong in the methods: multisite expert readers reached only substantial (not perfect) agreement (Fleiss' kappa 0.70-0.87), with calibration necessary for comparability [10].
  • Lesion handling must be reported as a method, not a footnote: lesion filling improved the power to detect atrophy and ventricular growth and reduced the required sample size by ~25% [19]; SynthSeg's value is precisely that it generalises without retraining, which must be stated together with the version used [20].
  • Multiple comparisons across many regions need a pre-specified correction: permutation inference for the GLM gives exact false-positive control under exchangeability [21]; the correction family and the number of tested regions must be reported [1, 3].

Practical recommendation (as written for a multisite longitudinal stroke cohort)

Two-column checklist: what to report, and what our cohort must document concretely.

# Item Source What our cohort must document
1 Study design / cohort checklist STROBE [5, 6] Prospective cohort, eligibility, recruitment, visit windows for 0/6/36/60 months, flow diagram with attrition per visit and per site
2 Imaging acquisition reporting (COBIDAS) [1, 2, 3] MPRAGE 1 mm parameters, the exact sequence per site, all Siemens models by site, field strength, coil, protocol deviations, scan dates
3 Preprocessing reporting [1, 2, 3] SynthSeg 2 / FreeSurfer 8.2, exact flags (--robust --qc), cross-sectional per-session processing (state explicitly that no longitudinal stream was used), recon-all for thickness, container/image digest
4 Software version freezing [1, 2, 4, 20] Frozen versions and container hashes for segmentation, recon-all, QC gate, lesion filling, harmonisation and model code; repository DOI
5 Lesion filling as a method [19, 20] Infarct filling by contralateral mirroring and WMH filling with NAWM intensity described operationally; which analysis is primary (raw vs filled); agreement/validation of the filled images
6 SVD terminology STRIVE-2 [8] WMH and atrophy defined in STRIVE-2 terms; acknowledge that WMH delineation conventionally uses FLAIR and that a T1-only cohort limits WMH quantification
7 QC metric and gate [11, 12, 13] SynthSeg per-structure QC >= 0.65 and Euler-number gate defined a priori, per-site and per-structure counts excluded, and the gate applied before any model fit
8 QC beyond the gate [12, 13] Sensitivity analysis with QC score as covariate; per-site QC distributions; statement that automated QC generalises imperfectly to new sites
9 Blinding / rating independence [10] QC and any manual lesion editing blind to group, visit and site; if any manual step remains: rater training, number of raters, inter-rater agreement
10 Statistical model [15, 22] Linear mixed models fully specified (fixed effects, random intercepts/slopes, time metric, covariance structure), and explicit separation of between- and within-subject effects
11 Multiple comparisons [1, 3, 21] Pre-specified region family, correction method (e.g. permutation with randomise), one-/two-sided, and the number of tested structures
12 Pre-registration / analysis plan [7, 22] Dated analysis plan before analysis (OSF/registered report): hypotheses, region set, primary outcome, covariates, correction, sensitivity/multiverse analyses
13 Multisite harmonisation [14, 15, 16, 17, 18] longComBat specification (batch = site/scanner, covariates, time); pre-harmonisation scanner-variance quantification; a within-subject scanner-change sensitivity analysis because longitudinal harmonisation failed in direct comparison
14 Missing data / attrition [5, 6] Missingness pattern at 36/60 months (death, dropout, scanner change), assumed mechanism, and the model's handling of it
15 Robustness of the segmentation tool [20] Statement that SynthSeg generalises across contrast/resolution without retraining, plus the version actually used and its QC behaviour in our data
16 Data and code sharing [1, 2, 4] Sharing statement: derived data, analysis code, container, and what cannot be shared (patient-level imaging) and why
