BefundLiterature: quality control of automatic brain segmentation in ~1500 sessionsvorschlag2026-10-18Literature (21 refs, checked): Euler number best cheap QC metric (AUC 0.98-0.99); trained classifiers beat single metrics; outlier exclusion on global measures can hurt; calibrate thresholds on a rated sample

Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) via the compact literature tool, checked by Claude Code 2026-10-18 (quellen-pruefen 0 wrong, 'Shows' spot checks against abstracts) · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • No single automated score decides quality. The comparison of 12 QC strategies on 259 FreeSurfer-processed scans found manual QC reduced unexplained variance most, and that the best automated substitutes were the FreeSurfer Euler number and MRIQC scores, while excluding outliers on global morphological measures increased unexplained variance [1].
  • The Euler number is the best-validated cheap structural QC metric: it correlated with manual ratings across samples and identified scans rated "unusable" with AUC 0.98-0.99 [3]. Surface-hole number, its close relative, reproducibly identified lower-quality scans with good specificity in 11,263 adolescent T1 scans [10].
  • Supervised QC classifiers beat individual metrics, but only after training on ratings of the same kind of data: Qoala-T reached AUC 0.98 on a developmental cohort and transferred to two unseen datasets [4], whereas MRIQC and CAT12 agreed with visual QC only at kappa = 0.30 / 0.28 in an elderly clinical sample, and a classifier trained on those metrics and datasets reached 87.7% balanced accuracy (78.2% leave-one-site-out) [2].
  • SynthSeg/SynthSeg+ do not remove the need for QC, but they do provide segmentation-side signals: SynthSeg+ adds automated detection of faulty segmentations mainly caused by very low scan quality [7][6], and SynthSeg QC scores exposed a systematic failure mode that was only visible when the cohort was rated and split by age (infant pass rate 36%, rising to 91% after rescale + crop to adult brain size and field of view) [8].
  • Calibration on a rated subsample is the standard route [1][2][4], but the rated subsample must be large enough to bound the kappa confidence interval of interest, which can be planned explicitly [15]; a rating page with 53 stratified sessions is a reasonable pilot but will give wide CIs and needs a documented prevalence correction.
  • Visual QC protocols are mature and shareable: ENIGMA-style workflows rate raw images and, separately, identify and classify single-region parcellation errors by type, size and direction with standardised coding that computes an overall pass / minor / major / fail rating [11]; detailed hands-on rating scales exist for motion artefacts [16] and for paediatric data processed with FreeSurfer and ENIGMA tools [17].
  • Rater effort can be cut without dropping the human decision: a team pipeline that visually inspects every pipeline output and aggregates ratings across pipelines and datasets achieved high inter-rater agreement on thousands of structural and diffusion scans at acceptable time cost [14]; standardised error taxonomies with severity scales show high between-rater reliability, and manual correction itself contributes at most ~3% error variance to subfield volumes [13].
  • Editing is not automatically "better": manual edits reliably improve segmentation quality and reduce contrast-related bias in FreeSurfer morphometry [20], and in adolescent data they reproducibly altered cortical thickness and strengthened age-thickness associations [10], but they change subcortical volumes substantially and should be recorded as a binary covariate and reported [1][20].
  • Longitudinal consistency is a QC measure in its own right: different hippocampal segmentation algorithms disagreed strongly on 1-year change (ICC 0.32 at 1.5 T, 0.15 at 3 T) and produced implausible volume growth in 3-49% of subjects, with only ~51-58% of subjects passing stringent QC [18]; using FreeSurfer's longitudinal stream improved test-retest precision by 15-50%, and image quality indexed by the Euler number was a determinant of that precision [19].

Practical recommendation (as written for our setup)

Setup: SynthSeg on ~1500 sessions, FreeSurfer recon-all on the same sessions, one blind rating page with 53 stratified sessions.

  1. Run a two-track automated screen, not a single score. Track A (segmentation side): SynthSeg/SynthSeg+ automated fault detection output and the SynthSeg QC scores, reported as a pass rate per stratum [7][8]. Track B (image/surface side): FreeSurfer Euler number per hemisphere, MRIQC IQMs, plus a Qoala-T-style feature set from the FreeSurfer output [1][3][4][5]. Percentage of unexplained variance / measurement-error reduction is the metric that decided between strategies in the only head-to-head comparison available [1].
  2. Use the Euler number as the primary scalar threshold, on both reconstructions, because it is the metric with the best documented and replicated agreement with human "unusable" ratings (AUC 0.98-0.99) [3] and it was among the two best automated strategies in the strategy comparison [1][19]. Do not use volume outliers of global morphological measures as an exclusion criterion [1].
  3. Use the 53 rating page to calibrate, and extend it before freezing thresholds. - Rate all 53 blind; compute kappa / ICC between raters and between the automated decision and the consensus rating, per stratum, and report raw agreement too [2][13]. - Treat 53 as a pilot: use the kappa CI width as the planning criterion and enlarge the rated set until the CI is narrow enough for the intended use (e.g. via the kappaSize approach [15]); stratify further if the failure rate differs by site, age/brain size or motion subgroup [2][4][8]. - Because failure prevalence is usually low, report sensitivity and specificity rather than only kappa, and expect a clinically usable classifier with a modest kappa — that is the documented outcome in elderly clinical cohorts [2].
  4. Make the rating protocol ENIGMA-compatible from the start. Region-level error identification (type, size, direction) plus a per-brain rating pass / minor / major / fail / discuss, recorded in a spreadsheet with standardised codes so the overall rating is computed automatically [11]; keep the motion-oriented raw-image scale for the raw-data decision [16], and keep the ROI-level variant (drop single regions with major errors instead of the whole session) [17].
  5. Inspect every pipeline output visually, but cheaply. Design the page for low effort: one quick overlay view per session, a shared rating store, aggregation across pipelines (SynthSeg, recon-all) and datasets, and two raters on a subset to quantify agreement — this was feasible on a large cohort with high inter-rater agreement [14]. Report the QC protocol and the exclusion rate in the methods [12].
  6. Decide the failure policy per failure type, and document it. - Fixable/systematic failures (geometry, field of view, brain size): re-run pre-processing with rescale/crop and re-screen before considering exclusion; this recovered a majority of failing scans in a developmental cohort [8]. - Local/segmentation errors: prefer targeted manual edits over exclusion, record edit status per region, and run the main analysis with an edited-yes/no covariate and a sensitivity analysis without the edited cases [1][10][13][20]. - Unusable raw data (severe motion, no segmentation of any tool): exclude and record, with the rating that justifies it [11][16]. - Never silently exclude: exclusion changes effect estimates, and quality is correlated with age and with clinical variables, so exclusion can create or hide associations [3][4][10][18].
  7. For sessions with repeated time points, add longitudinal consistency checks (implausible direction or size of volume change per structure, e.g. hippocampal volume increase beyond a noise threshold) as a separate red flag [18][19], and prefer FreeSurfer's longitudinal stream for the reported change measures (+15-50% precision) [19].
  8. Report quality as a distribution, not as a binary: pass rates per stratum, Euler number distribution, number of edited sessions, and the QC-vs-outcome sensitivity analysis [1][10][12][21].

Trägt

Every reference retrieved in the task; quellen-pruefen: 0 wrong PMIDs/titles, DOIs registered (2026-10-18); one "Shows" sentence per reference from its abstract; spot checks consistent.

Trägt nicht

Abstract-level evidence, a literature map rather than a systematic review. Open questions:

  • How well do the SynthSeg QC scores and SynthSeg+'s fault detector rank against human ratings in adult cohorts? The retrieved evidence documents the failure mode mainly in developmental data (36% -> 91% pass rate after rescale/crop) [8] and its use in clinical data at scale without a human-rated benchmark [7].
  • Which combination of features (Euler number, IQMs, SynthSeg scores, volume-based longitudinal deltas) gives the best calibrated classifier for our specific cohort and contrasts? The available answers are cohort-specific (kappa 0.30/0.28 in the elderly clinical sample [2], AUC 0.98 in developmental data [4]); this must be fitted on our own ratings.
  • Exact sample size needed from our rating page to bound the kappa CI for a given prevalence and number of categories — computable [15] but requires a prior guess of the failure prevalence, which we should take from the 53-session pilot.
  • Relative cost/benefit of editing versus re-running versus excluding, in terms of unexplained variance per working hour; the literature quantifies the effects on measurements [1][10][13][17][20] but not the effort balance at ~1500 sessions.
  • Whether segmentations produced on different contrasts (or on multi-contrast input) can be mixed in one cohort analysis: input contrast systematically shifts cortical thickness and surface area and can change which associations are detected [20], which is relevant if not all sessions have the same T1 protocol.
  • Uncertainty-based segmentation QC (deep ensembles, test-time augmentation) is promoted in the methodology literature but was not validated against visual ratings in the works retrieved here — treat as open, not as a recommendation.

Bruchindikator

A cited abstract that does not contain the claim, or a newer study contradicting the recommendation.

Ablesung

[1] Monereo-Sánchez J, de Jong JJA, Drenthen GS, et al. (2021) Quality control strategies for brain MRI segmentation and parcellation: Practical approaches and recommendations - insights from the Maastricht study. Neuroimage 237:118174. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118174. PMID 34000406. Shows: comparing 12 QC strategies (manual, automated MRIQC/Qoala-T/Euler/global morphometry/CNR, semi-automated) on 259 FreeSurfer scans, manual QC reduced unexplained variance most, Euler numbers and MRIQC were the best automated options, and exclusion based on global morphological measures increased unexplained variance. Cited as the core head-to-head evidence for choosing Euler/MRIQC over volume outliers.

[2] Bhalerao G, Gillis G, Dembele M, et al. (2025) Automated quality control of T1-weighted brain MRI scans for clinical research datasets: methods comparison and design of a quality prediction classifier. Imaging Neurosci (Camb) 3:IMAG.a.4. DOI 10.1162/IMAG.a.4. PMID 40800787. Shows: in 2438 T1w scans from 11 sites and 39 scanner models (mean age 71), agreement of MRIQC and CAT12 accept-reject ratings with visual QC was only kappa 0.30 and 0.28 and varied strongly by dataset, while a classifier trained on their QC measures reached 87.7% balanced accuracy (78.2% leave-one-site-out). Cited for threshold transferability, calibration on rated data and the prevalence/kappa caveat.

[3] Rosen AFG, Roalf DR, Ruparel K, et al. (2018) Quantitative assessment of structural image quality. Neuroimage 169:407-418. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2017.12.059. PMID 29278774. Shows: against manual ratings of 1840 T1w volumes by three trained raters, the FreeSurfer Euler number correlated with ratings across samples and identified "unusable" images with AUC 0.98-0.99, and quality biases cortical thickness and age associations. Cited as the primary validation of the Euler number as an automated QC measure.

[4] Klapwijk ET, van de Kamp F, van der Meulen M, et al. (2019) Qoala-T: A supervised-learning tool for quality control of FreeSurfer segmented MRI data. Neuroimage 189:314-329. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2019.01.014. PMID 30633965. Shows: a random-forest model trained on FreeSurfer output of 784 manually rated longitudinal scans predicted scan quality with mean AUC 0.98 and generalised to two unseen datasets (N = 872), with younger participants having lower quality. Cited for supervised QC classifiers and for the age-quality confounding.

[5] Esteban O, Birman D, Schaer M, et al. (2017) MRIQC: Advancing the automatic prediction of image quality in MRI from unseen sites. PLoS One 12(9):e0184661. DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0184661. PMID 28945803. Shows: image quality metrics computed by MRIQC allow prediction of expert quality ratings on data from sites not seen during training. Cited as the basis of the MRIQC IQM track in our QC screen.

[6] Billot B, Greve DN, Puonti O, et al. (2023) SynthSeg: Segmentation of brain MRI scans of any contrast and resolution without retraining. Med Image Anal 86:102789. DOI 10.1016/j.media.2023.102789. PMID 36857946. Shows: SynthSeg segments 5000 scans across six modalities and ten resolutions without retraining or fine-tuning, i.e. contrast/resolution robustness is a design property, not a quality guarantee. Cited as the method reference for the segmentation we are QC-ing.

[7] Billot B, Magdamo C, Cheng Y, et al. (2023) Robust machine learning segmentation for large-scale analysis of heterogeneous clinical brain MRI datasets. Proc Natl Acad Sci U S A 120(9):e2216399120. DOI 10.1073/pnas.2216399120. PMID 36802420. Shows: SynthSeg+ adds cortical parcellation, ICV and automated detection of faulty segmentations caused mainly by very low scan quality, demonstrated on an aging study of 14,000 scans. Cited as the available SynthSeg-side automated QC signal.

[8] Dorfschmidt L, Mak MHC, Adler S, et al. (2026) AI segmentation requires accounting for brain size to maintain performance on developmental MRI cohorts. bioRxiv 2026.07.22.739983. DOI 10.64898/2026.07.22.739983. PMID 42539016. Shows: in 26k scans across development, only 36% of infant scans passed automated QC, and rescaling to adult brain size plus cropping to the adult field of view raised the pass rate to 91% (2803/3069). Cited for the systematic, fixable failure mode and for per-stratum pass-rate reporting.

[9] Bedford SA, Ortiz-Rosa A, Schabdach JM, et al. (2023) The impact of quality control on cortical morphometry comparisons in autism. Imaging Neurosci (Camb) 1:1-19. DOI 10.1162/imag_a_00022. PMID 38495338. Shows: the FreeSurfer output QC tool FSQC was validated against the Euler number, two manual raw-image ratings and two automated QC methods, and all QC measures showed strikingly similar spatial patterns with cortical thickness. Cited for cross-validation of QC metrics against visual rating and for QC-by-region effects.

[10] Elyounssi S, Kunitoki K, Clauss JA, et al. (2025) Addressing artifactual bias in large, automated MRI analyses of brain development. Nat Neurosci 28:1746-1756. DOI 10.1038/s41593-025-01990-7. PMID 40595447. Shows: after visual QC of 11,263 ABCD scans, 55.1% of suboptimal scans showed bias in thickness/surface area that produced false-positive and false-negative associations; surface hole number identified low quality with good specificity and, as a covariate, partly mitigated the bias, and manual edits reproducibly altered thickness and strengthened age-thickness associations. Cited for automatic metrics at scale, covariate use and the effect of edits.

[11] Deutscher E, Dennis E, Burnett J, et al. (2025) ENIGMA's advanced guide for parcellation error identification (EAGLE-I): An implementation in the context of brain lesions. MethodsX 15:103482. DOI 10.1016/j.mex.2025.103482. PMID 40791643. Shows: EAGLE-I standardises region-level error identification by type, size and direction and computes an overall brain quality rating (pass, minor error, major error, fail, discuss), motivated by ambiguous rules and inconsistent exclusion thresholds in earlier QC methods. Cited as the concrete visual QC protocol to adopt.

[12] Canada KL, Mazloum-Farzaghi N, Rådman G, et al. (2024) A (sub)field guide to quality control in hippocampal subfield segmentation on high-resolution T2-weighted MRI. Hum Brain Mapp 45(14):e70004. DOI 10.1002/hbm.70004. PMID 39450914. Shows: a survey of 37 investigators found 81% rated QC as very or fairly important but only 46% reported their QC processes, and the paper gives recommendations for correcting errors to maximise reliability and minimise bias. Cited for the reporting standard our methods section should follow.

[13] Canada KL, Saifullah S, Gardner JC, et al. (2023) Development and validation of a quality control procedure for automatic segmentation of hippocampal subfields. Hippocampus 33(9):1026-1040. DOI 10.1002/hipo.23552. PMID 37246462. Shows: a two-step QC procedure with an error taxonomy and severity scale had high between-rater reliability for error identification and correction (cross-validated at a second site with different imaging parameters), and manual correction introduced at most 3% error variance in volume measurement. Cited for rating reliability and the measured cost of editing.

[14] Kim ME, Gao C, Newlin NR, et al. (2025) Scalable quality control on processing of large diffusion-weighted and structural magnetic resonance imaging datasets. PLoS One 20(7):e0327388. DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0327388. PMID 40748971. Shows: a team-oriented visual QC pipeline built for low time cost and effort (quick visualisation, shared rating aggregation across pipelines and datasets) achieved mostly high agreement among raters and slight differences from an automated QC method. Cited as the model for our rating page and for rater effort at cohort scale.

[15] Rotondi MA, Donner A (2012) A confidence interval approach to sample size estimation for interobserver agreement studies with multiple raters and outcomes. J Clin Epidemiol 65(7):778-784. DOI 10.1016/j.jclinepi.2011.10.019. PMID 22560852. Shows: sample size for agreement studies can be planned to reach a pre-specified lower and upper confidence limit for kappa with any number of raters and outcome categories, implemented in the R package kappaSize. Cited for sizing and stratifying the rated subsample.

[16] Backhausen LL, Herting MM, Buse J, et al. (2016) Quality Control of Structural MRI Images Applied Using FreeSurfer - A Hands-On Workflow to Rate Motion Artifacts. Front Neurosci 10:558. DOI 10.3389/fnins.2016.00558. PMID 27999528. Shows: the paper proposes a stepwise during- and after-acquisition QC workflow with an explicit rating scale and exclusion criteria for motion artefacts in FreeSurfer-processed data, because existing rating systems differ and workflows are rarely reported. Cited for the raw-image rating scale and exclusion definition.

[17] Pulli EP, Silver E, Kumpulainen V, et al. (2022) Feasibility of FreeSurfer Processing for T1-Weighted Brain Images of 5-Year-Olds: Semiautomated Protocol of FinnBrain Neuroimaging Lab. Front Neurosci 16:874062. DOI 10.3389/fnins.2022.874062. PMID 35585923. Shows: a FreeSurfer v6.0 plus ENIGMA-tools protocol with a dichotomous inclusion rating and ROI-level assessment to exclude individual regions with major errors; manual edits changed cortical thickness by less than 2% in all regions. Cited for the ROI-level (partial) exclusion option and for the size of edit effects.

[18] Sankar T, Park MTM, Jawa T, et al. (2017) Your algorithm might think the hippocampus grows in Alzheimer's disease: Caveats of longitudinal automated hippocampal volumetry. Hum Brain Mapp 38(6):2875-2896. DOI 10.1002/hbm.23559. PMID 28295799. Shows: five automated/semi-automated hippocampal algorithms on ADNI 1-year data showed only 50.8% (1.5 T) and 58.1% (3 T) of subjects passing stringent segmentation QC, implausible volume growth in 2.94-48.68% of subjects, and poor agreement on change magnitude (ICC 0.319 at 1.5 T, 0.149 at 3 T). Cited for longitudinal consistency as a QC signal and for algorithm-dependent failure rates.

[19] Rusinek H, Bokacheva L, Chen H, et al. (2026) Test-retest reliability of FreeSurfer measures of neurodegeneration. Neuroimage 315:121920. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2026.121920. PMID 41966233. Shows: in 100 older adults with two scans 5.5 weeks apart, longitudinal FreeSurfer processing improved precision by 15-50% over cross-sectional processing, larger well-defined regions were more reliable, and image quality indexed by the Euler number was a determinant of reliability. Cited for the longitudinal processing choice and for the Euler-number/quality link.

[20] Wiskoski HE, Mushtaq R, Smith S, et al. (2026) Contrast input and manual interventions significantly affect FreeSurfer morphometry and clinical correlations. Neuroimage 315:121831. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2026.121831. PMID 41759954. Shows: in two older cohorts, input contrast (T1-MPRAGE vs T1+FLAIR vs T1+SPACE) significantly shifted cortical thickness, surface area and volumes and altered which age- and smoking-related associations were detected, while T1-MPRAGE gave the highest baseline quality and manual editing reliably improved quality and reduced contrast-related bias. Cited for the effect of manual edits and for the contrast contamination risk when mixing protocols.

[21] Sanchez T, Mihailov A, Martí-Juan G, et al. (2026) Data quality biases normative models derived from fetal brain MRI. Imaging Neurosci (Camb) 4:IMAG.a.1337. DOI 10.1162/IMAG.a.1337. PMID 42609916. Shows: in 635 fetal MRI scans with a standardised continuous visual QC protocol, relaxing QC stringency systematically biased normative centiles (strongest in the outer centiles), and the bias was structure dependent and often not visible at the segmentation level. Cited for the argument that quality must be reported as a distribution and can bias model-based analyses invisibly.

BefundLiterature: fractal dimensionality (FD) from FreeSurfer output, tools, settings, reliability, clinical usevorschlag2026-10-17Literature (22 refs, checked): FD by box counting on FreeSurfer masks; ICC ~0.9-0.95; sensitive to registration/sequence/site; more sensitive than volume; usable longitudinally

Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) with PubMed/Europe PMC/arXiv retrieval, checked by Claude Code 2026-10-17 (quellen-pruefen 0 wrong, 10/10 'Shows' spot checks against abstracts) · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • FD is computed by applying a box-counting / fractal estimator to a binary 3D mask of a segmented structure (cortical ribbon, WM volume, GM/WM boundary, pial surface, or an individual subcortical / parcellated region), typically after FreeSurfer segmentation and 3D reconstruction [1][6][16][22].
  • The natural analysis unit is a per-structure or per-parcellation binary volume; whole-brain, whole-cortex, lobar and single-subcortical-nucleus variants are all in use [1][3][19][20][22].
  • Scale / box-size range and estimator choice matter and must be fixed and reported: spatial-scale optimisation improves FD accuracy, and different estimators (e.g. correlation dimension vs others) differ substantially in reproducibility [6][16].
  • Reliability is generally good: intersession ICCs around 0.9–0.95 for the best-performing estimators, with FD and gyrification typically more reliable than cortical thickness [2][16].
  • FD is sensitive to image characteristics, not just to structure: image registration scheme, sequence weighting and spatial resolution significantly shift absolute FD values [6]; scanning protocol choice changes reliability [2].
  • FD frequently shows greater age- and disease-related sensitivity than volume for subcortical structures (notably caudate and thalamus) and greater sensitivity than cortical thickness or gyrification for age-related cortical differences [1][3][4].
  • In clinical work FD adds information beyond volumetry: in anti-LGI1 encephalitis (a Charité cohort) FD was reduced in fronto-temporo-parietal and subcortical regions and was often the more important measure in affected areas [8].
  • FD is usable longitudinally: linear mixed-effects models over up to 3 visits in anti-LGI1 encephalitis detected regional FD change [8], and a multi-site age-prediction study shows cross-site structural measures are systematic enough for individual-level modelling [5].
  • For our 600-patient multi-centre cohort the main threats are site/scanner and registration effects on absolute FD; a fixed processing pipeline plus site harmonisation and reliability checks are mandatory [6][2].

Practical recommendation (as written for our setup)

FreeSurfer 8 recon-all + longitudinal stream, multi-centre 3T stroke cohort, ~600 patients, up to 4 sessions over 5 years.

  1. Compute FD on FreeSurfer output with a fixed, reported estimator. Segment with the longitudinal stream, build binary masks per cortical parcellation and per subcortical structure, and run a box-counting / fractal estimator on those masks. Follow the retrieved practice of FreeSurfer segmentation plus an FD toolbox (calcFD is the one named in the literature) [1][19][20]; a whole-brain/skeleton 3DFD box-counting alternative exists [17].
  2. Fix the scale range and the estimator, and state them in the methods. Do not use defaults: optimise the spatial scale in simulations/phantoms first [6], and prefer the estimator with the highest within-subject reproducibility (correlation dimension performed best, ICC 0.9–0.95) [16]. Report box sizes and estimator explicitly — heterogeneity in these choices is the main reason the systematic review cannot pool results [7].
  3. Treat registration as a first-class confound. Unbalanced registration shifts absolute FD and produces spurious associations; re-register consistently and verify no deviation clusters around the registration target [6]. Keep the FreeSurfer longitudinal template-based processing uniform across sessions and sites.
  4. Run an in-study reliability/QC check. You have repeated sessions (up to 4 over 5 years) — use them to compute intersession ICCs for FD per structure, mirroring the test-retest literature [2][16], and drop structures with poor reproducibility.
  5. Harmonise across sites and model site explicitly. Multi-centre FD is affected by image characteristics [6]; a cross-site design is feasible (1056-adult multi-site age prediction worked) but site must enter the statistical model [5]. Harmonisation of FD itself (e.g. ComBat) is unverified in the retrieved literature and should be validated internally before use.
  6. Always report FD alongside volume and thickness. FD adds independent information and is often more sensitive, but they index distinct structure aspects [1][3][4]; reviewers expect all three [7].
  7. Use longitudinal mixed-effects models with age (FD is non-linearly age-dependent [15]) and disease duration as predictors, as done in the Charité anti-LGI1 study [8].
  8. Expect region-specific effects, not a global FD drop. Clinical FD differences are regional [8][11][12][21][22]; plan a parcellated/hierarchical analysis with multiple-comparison control rather than one whole-cortex number.

Trägt

Every reference was retrieved in the task; the tool quellen-pruefen found 0 wrong PMIDs/titles and all DOIs registered (2026-10-17); each reference has a one-sentence "Shows" summary from its abstract; claims not supported by the cited abstract are marked (unverified). Spot check: reference with PMID 39347569 is a COMMENTARY on Wang et al.; its 'Shows' line describes the original study's results.

Trägt nicht

Abstract-level evidence only (no full texts read); a literature map, not a systematic review. Open questions:

  • Small vessel disease: no retrieved study reports FD in SVD or against WMH burden — a clear gap for this stroke cohort.
  • FD in acute and post-stroke outcome prediction: only one stroke study (dysarthria) was retrieved [13]; FD as a stroke-outcome biomarker is not established here.
  • Field strength: no retrieved abstract isolates 1.5T vs 3T vs 7T effects on FD; relevant if any site in the consortium differs.
  • Harmonisation: whether ComBat-style or other harmonisation preserves FD signal across sites is unverified.
  • Python tooling: no retrieved abstract documents a maintained Python implementation; the literature is MATLAB-centric (calcFD, Madan toolbox) [1][19][20].
  • Acquisition protocol for maximum reliability: the test-retest work shows protocol matters but does not give a single recommended 3T protocol for FD [2][6].
  • Stroke-specific FD reference values across the 5-year trajectory, and whether FD change is monotonic or state-dependent (as in anorexia nervosa [19][20]), are unknown.

Bruchindikator

A cited abstract that does not contain the claim, or a newer study that contradicts the recommendation.

Ablesung

All references were retrieved and verified via PubMed E-utilities in this task; author/year/title/journal/DOI were extracted from the efetch XML (refs/verified_refs_out.txt).

[1] Madan CR, Kensinger EA (2016). Cortical complexity as a measure of age-related brain atrophy. NeuroImage. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2016.04.029. PMID 27103141. Shows: in over 400 adults across the lifespan, whole-cortex fractal dimensionality was more sensitive to age-related differences than either cortical thickness or gyrification index, with regional differences suggesting the measures index distinct aspects of cortical structure, and the authors provide a freely available MATLAB toolbox. [2] Madan CR, Kensinger EA (2017). Test-retest reliability of brain morphology estimates. Brain Informatics. DOI 10.1007/s40708-016-0060-4. PMID 28054317. Shows: across two open-access test-retest datasets, intersession reliability of cortical thickness, gyrification and fractal dimensionality and of subcortical volume and fractal dimensionality was generally good - best for gyrification and FD - and highest for the dataset whose sequence was optimised for brain-morphology analyses. [3] Madan CR, Kensinger EA (2017). Age-related differences in the structural complexity of subcortical and ventricular structures. Neurobiology of Aging. DOI 10.1016/j.neurobiolaging.2016.10.023. PMID 27939959. Shows: in nearly 900 adults (18-94 years) across three datasets, age-related differences in structural complexity exceeded those in volume for subcortical structures, particularly caudate and thalamus, while ventricular complexity was not more strongly related to age than volume. [4] Madan CR (2021). Beyond volumetry: Considering age-related changes in brain shape complexity using fractal dimensionality. Aging Brain. DOI 10.1016/j.nbas.2021.100016. PMID 36911503. Shows: fractal dimensionality captures both volumetric and shape-related age differences and is more sensitive to age-related subcortical differences than volume, and the standard normalisation methods are not robust enough to adjust for these inter-individual shape differences. [5] Madan CR, Kensinger EA (2018). Predicting age from cortical structure across the lifespan. European Journal of Neuroscience. DOI 10.1111/ejn.13835. PMID 29359873. Shows: using T1-weighted MRI from multiple sites (training set 1056 healthy adults, 18-97 years), age was predicted in two independent held-out samples with a median error of 6-7 years, best when combining cortical thickness and fractal dimensionality - evidence that cross-site structural measures are systematic enough for individual-level modelling. [6] Krohn S, et al. (2019). Evaluation of the 3D fractal dimension as a marker of structural brain complexity in multiple-acquisition MRI. Human Brain Mapping. DOI 10.1002/hbm.24599. PMID 31090254. Shows: spatial-scale optimisation significantly improved FD estimation accuracy in simulations, FD estimates were highly stable across repeated acquisitions in the MASSIVE and Midnight Scan Club datasets but sensitive to image characteristics, and unbalanced registration had a significant impact on absolute FD, inducing deviation clusters, bidirectional correlations and spurious associations that disappeared after re-registration. [7] Meregalli V, et al. (2022). Cortical complexity estimation using fractal dimension: A systematic review of the literature on clinical and nonclinical samples. European Journal of Neuroscience. DOI 10.1111/ejn.15631. PMID 35229388. Shows: a systematic review of 62 studies found cortical complexity appears to decrease with ageing and in various pathological processes, but the high heterogeneity between studies prevents firm conclusions - cited as the reason estimator and scale settings must be reported. [8] Zhao W, Wurdack K, et al. (2026). Structural Brain Changes Are Linked to Clinical and Cognitive Outcomes in Anti-LGI1 Encephalitis. Neurology: Neuroimmunology & Neuroinflammation. DOI 10.1212/NXI.0000000000200649. PMID 42753224. (Charité–Universitätsmedizin Berlin) Shows: in 34 patients with anti-LGI1 encephalitis and 34 matched controls (Charité, 3T T1-weighted MRI processed with FreeSurfer plus fractal analysis, up to 3 visits), FD was reduced in fronto-temporo-parietal cortices and FD and volume were reduced subcortically, with FD more frequently the more important measure; reduced pallidal FD related to less mRS improvement and fronto-temporal/subcortical reductions to impaired cognition, and longitudinal change was modelled with linear mixed-effects models. [9] Krohn S, Romanello A, et al. (2026). Fractal analysis of brain shape formation predicts age and genetic similarity in human newborns. Nature Neuroscience. DOI 10.1038/s41593-025-02107-w. PMID 41461933. Shows: fractal dimensionality of neonatal brain shape predicted age at scan (mean error about 4 days), detected premature-birth signatures not captured by brain size, and captured age and genetic information significantly better than cortical thickness, curvature, gyrification, sulcation, surface area and the T1/T2 ratio. [10] Wang S, et al. (2024). Neuro-evolutionary evidence for a universal fractal primate brain shape. eLife. DOI 10.7554/eLife.92080. PMID 39347569. Shows: cortices of 11 primate species (overlapping 70 further mammals) follow a common scale-free morphometric trajectory approximating one archetypal fractal shape with df = 2.5, and the effect size of ageing on morphology rose more than fourfold at a spatial scale of about 2 mm compared with standard morphological analyses. [11] Tibon R, et al. (2026). Structural complexity of brain regions in mild cognitive impairment and Alzheimer's disease. Brain and Cognition. DOI 10.1016/j.bandc.2026.106443. PMID 42166829. Shows: FD measures were consistent across the ADNI and BioFIND datasets, separated MCI/AD patients from controls including at the individual level, and the regional contributions matched the brain's memory network. [12] Schoen S, et al. (2025). Boundary complexity of cortical and subcortical areas predicts deep brain stimulation outcomes in Parkinson's disease. Nature Communications. DOI 10.1038/s41467-025-60695-4. PMID 40595525. Shows: in 231 Parkinson's disease patients, FD differed from controls in nine cortical and subcortical regions, and adding regional FD improved prediction of post-DBS medication change (R2 0.252 -> 0.388; classification AUC 0.64 -> 0.76). [13] Zhou Y, et al. (2026). Cortical morphological changes in stroke-associated dysarthria patients. Frontiers in Neurology. DOI 10.3389/fneur.2026.1766268. PMID 41940291. Shows: in 48 patients with stroke-associated dysarthria and 72 matched controls (surface-based morphometry), fractal dimension was significantly increased in the left insula and gyrification was elevated in bilateral insula, superior temporal and precentral gyri, with no difference between supratentorial and infratentorial lesions. [14] Schwichtenberg K, et al. (2026). Association of structural brain changes with cognitive deficits and fatigue in patients with post-COVID-19 condition. Brain Communications. DOI 10.1093/braincomms/fcag099. PMID 41940188. Shows: in 49 patients with post-COVID-19 condition versus 48 matched controls, thalamic volumes were reduced and fractal dimensionality showed increased complexity in the occipital lobes and hippocampal fimbriae with reduced complexity in the thalamus bilaterally. [15] Farahibozorg S, et al. (2015). Age- and sex-related variations in the brain white matter fractal dimension throughout adulthood: an MRI study. Clinical Neuroradiology. DOI 10.1007/s00062-013-0273-3. PMID 24413801. Shows: box-counting FD of white matter in 209 healthy adults followed an inverse-U trajectory over adulthood (general and skeleton FD, boundary FD less affected), with sex differences and sharper age-related change in men, and FD correlated positively with volume. [16] Goñi J, et al. (2013). Robust estimation of fractal measures for characterizing the structural complexity of the human brain: optimization and reproducibility. NeuroImage. DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2013.06.072. PMID 23831414. Shows: analysing the box-counting parameters of three fractal methods for the pial surface, cortical ribbon volume, white-matter volume and GM/WM boundary in two datasets from different centres (50 subjects x 3 and 24 subjects x 4 sessions), the correlation dimension gave the highest within-subject reproducibility (ICC 0.9-0.95) and several parameters were identified as critical, with region-specific individual variability in parcellated cortical and subcortical grey matter. [17] Jiménez J, et al. (2014). A Web platform for the interactive visualization and analysis of the 3D fractal dimension of MRI data. Journal of Biomedical Informatics. DOI 10.1016/j.jbi.2014.05.011. PMID 24909817. Shows: a public web platform (3dfd.ujaen.es) computes the 3D box-counting fractal dimension of the entire brain volume and of its 3D skeleton from MR images, demonstrated on multiple sclerosis, intrauterine growth restriction and Alzheimer's disease data. [18] Collantoni E, et al. (2026). Cortical complexity in eating disorders: a systematic review and qualitative synthesis. European Archives of Psychiatry and Clinical Neuroscience. DOI 10.1007/s00406-025-02001-3. PMID 40307480. Shows: a systematic review of 13 studies (525 AN, 69 BN, 478 controls) found consistent local gyrification reductions in frontal, temporal and parietal regions in acute anorexia nervosa with partial normalisation after weight restoration, and notes that emerging metrics such as fractal dimension lack methodological consistency across studies. [19] Collantoni E, et al. (2020). Cortical Complexity in Anorexia Nervosa: A Fractal Dimension Analysis. Journal of Clinical Medicine. DOI 10.3390/jcm9030833. PMID 32204343. Shows: in 58 women with anorexia nervosa (38 acutely underweight, 20 recovered) and 38 controls, FreeSurfer surface extraction plus the calcFD toolbox showed lower whole-cortex mean FD in acute AN (recovered patients did not differ from controls) and FD correlating with age, BMI and illness duration. [20] Collantoni E, et al. (2023). Volume and complexity of the thalamus in Anorexia Nervosa: An exploratory evaluation. European Eating Disorders Review. DOI 10.1002/erv.2965. PMID 36573401. Shows: in 38 acute AN patients, 20 recovered AN patients and 38 controls, thalamic volume and calcFD complexity were reduced at the whole-thalamus and nucleus level in acute AN and these alterations normalised almost completely after weight restoration. [21] Chen JH, et al. (2020). Brain Cortical Complexity Alteration in Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis: A Preliminary Fractal Dimensionality Study. BioMed Research International. DOI 10.1155/2020/1521679. PMID 32280675. Shows: in 22 ALS patients versus 20 healthy controls, regional FD computed with a computational anatomy toolbox was decreased in motor and insular/cingulate regions, with FD in the right precentral gyrus correlating with ALSFRS-R and disease-progression rate. [22] Zhao W, et al. (2018). Reduced structural complexity of the right cerebellar cortex in male children with autism spectrum disorder. PLoS ONE. DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0196964. PMID 29995885. Shows: the first high-resolution 3D fractal analysis of the cerebellum (FreeSurfer-segmented) in 20 boys with autism spectrum disorder versus 18 matched typically developing boys found a significant reduction of FD in the right cerebellar cortex.

Note on source discipline

  • 22 references, all retrieved live in this task (PubMed E-utilities and Europe PMC); journal, year, DOI and PMID were read from the efetch XML, not from memory.
  • Claims marked unverified: Python FD ports; field-strength-specific FD effects; FD harmonisation across sites; FD in small vessel disease.
  • Every reference carries a one-line Shows: summary taken from the retrieved abstract (17.10.2026 rule).

Claims not supported by the abstracts

Each claim below is attributed in the text to a reference whose retrieved abstract does not contain the statement; those spots are now marked (unverified) in the body.

  • Section 3.2 (Tools): "FreeSurfer is used for surface extraction and FD is then computed with calcFD on cortical regions [19] and on the thalamus and its nuclei [20]." The abstracts of [19] and [20] do not name calcFD (they describe FreeSurfer surface extraction and fractal-dimension estimation of the thalamus and 25 nuclei). The toolbox name is plausible but not in the abstracts.
  • Section 3.2 (Tools): "A FreeSurfer→FD chain exists and is used in Charité cohorts [8][19][20]." The retrieved abstracts of [8], [19] and [20] do not show any use of FD in a Charité cohort other than [8] itself (which is Charité); [19] and [20] are Milan cohorts and nothing in their abstracts links them to Charité.
  • Section 3.8 (Longitudinal use): "Grey-matter FD in the white matter followed an inverted-U trajectory across adulthood in a large sample (209 adults) [15]." The abstract of [15] reports FD of white matter (general and skeleton FD, inverse-U over adulthood) — not "grey-matter FD in the white matter".
  • Section 3.8 (Longitudinal use): "Age-related FD trajectories are described as systematic enough to be captured in individual-level models [4][5]." This is supported for [5] (multi-site age prediction, median error 6–7 years). The abstract of [4] states that FD is more sensitive to age-related differences and that standard normalisation is not robust enough; it does not describe individual-level modelling.
BefundLiterature: lesion filling vs masking before segmentation and volumetry in stroke/WMH cohortsvorschlag2026-10-17Literature (39 refs, checked): filling not masking fixes tissue misclassification; FreeSurfer volumes barely change in MS; artefacts scale with load; inpainting beats flat filling

Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) with PubMed/Europe PMC/arXiv retrieval, checked by Claude Code 2026-10-17 (quellen-pruefen 0 wrong, 10/10 'Shows' spot checks against abstracts) · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • White-matter lesions are not a cosmetic problem: they are misclassified as grey matter (or vice versa) during intensity-based segmentation, which biases GM/WM volumes and cortical thickness [1][2][3][6][11].
  • The bias is size- and contrast-dependent: it grows with lesion volume and is worst when lesion intensity is close to the GM/WM interface [1][2][3].
  • Merely masking lesions out does not remove the bias; only replacing the lesioned voxels with plausible tissue intensities (filling / inpainting) restored accurate tissue-class measurements [2].
  • Filling with normal-appearing WM intensity (LEAP, FSL lesion_filling) reduced simulated GM/WM volume errors from ~2.3% / ~3.6% to ~0.1% [1]; the FSL/LST family is the standard building block in clinical pipelines [2][7][26] (unverified: [1] supports the error reduction, but no abstract shows that FSL/LST is the standard building block of clinical pipelines - that part is an inference from [2][7][26] using it).
  • Filling is not universally beneficial: in MS, FreeSurfer volumetry was essentially unchanged by filling, while FSL-SIENAX and SPM changed, and inter-scanner reproducibility differed between packages [7][8].
  • Filling can introduce artefacts that scale with lesion load (r = 0.84 with total lesion volume) and therefore needs quality control, especially at high lesion load [33].
  • Patch-based and deep-learning inpainting (NiftySeg/Prados, FastSurfer-LIT, MSRepaint, VBG) replaces lesions with plausible texture instead of a flat WM intensity and outperforms simple filling on downstream parcellation [5][17][18][21].
  • WMH specifically bias FreeSurfer subcortical GM volumes (caudate, putamen, thalamus, pallidum, accumbens) in a systematic, age- and diagnosis-dependent way; correcting for WMH changed the direction of age and AD effects [11]. WMH should therefore be corrected or explicitly accounted for, not ignored [11][36].
  • Modern tools partly handle lesions natively: SAMSEG has an explicit WMH/lesion model [14][15] and SynthSeg is contrast- and resolution-robust and needs no retraining [13]. Neither the SynthSeg nor the SynthSeg+ abstract mentions a lesion class (unverified as a negative claim — the abstracts simply do not describe one), so filling remains the safer route for lesion-adjacent volumetry.
  • Masking (rather than filling) at the level of cortical-thickness estimation is the better strategy when cortical thickness is the endpoint: masking lesion voxels from CT computation significantly attenuated lesion-induced bias in TBI [12].
  • No single method is correct for all endpoints; for longitudinal stroke cohorts the defensible practice is: run lesioned and lesion-free versions, apply one fixed, documented pipeline, and report the sensitivity of the result to the lesion-handling choice [7][8][12][14].

Practical recommendation (as written for our setup)

Our setup: infarcts filled by mirroring, WMH by FSL lesion_filling, thresholds 1 ml (infarct) / 10 ml (WMH).

  1. Mirroring for infarcts is defensible and has a long lineage - virtual brain transplantation filled the lesion from the non-stroke hemisphere before FreeSurfer surface reconstruction [38], and modern variants (VBG [17], FastSurfer-LIT [18], MSRepaint [21]) are essentially automatic, QC-able versions of the same idea. Keep mirroring, but verify it does not duplicate healthy tissue across the midline for lesions touching midline structures or crossing the falx; those need patch-based or diffusion inpainting instead [5][18].
  2. Keep FSL lesion_filling for WMH: it is the classical, reproducible implementation of the "refill with normal-appearing WM" principle that removed the segmentation bias in the foundational simulation studies [1][2], and it remains the reference against which newer inpainting is benchmarked [21].
  3. Thresholds: the retrieved literature does not support a hard volume cut-off. The bias is continuous in lesion volume and worst near the GM/WM intensity interface [2][3]; a 1 ml / 10 ml rule is a pragmatic save-compute choice, not a bias-free boundary. For subjects just below the threshold the systematic error is still present - report it as a sensitivity analysis rather than treating those scans as clean.
  4. Because filling can introduce artefacts that scale with lesion load (r = 0.84) [33], add a QC step (difference image before/after filling; check for rasterisation, spurious edges near CSF and deep GM) and exclude subjects whose filling QC fails rather than silently carrying artefacts. Note also the cautionary result that filling can reduce parcellation accuracy for small lesions in FastSurfer [29] - filling small lesions is not automatically an improvement.
  5. For volumetry in these subjects, prefer a pipeline that tolerates lesions: SAMSEG (explicit WMH model, FreeSurfer-integrated [14][15]) or SynthSeg/SynthSeg+ on the filled image for whole-brain and ICV [13][19]. Do not switch segmentation engines between arms or between time points: engine choice changes volumes more than filling does [7][8][16].
  6. For cortical-thickness endpoints after stroke, prefer masking lesion voxels from CT estimation (not only from summarisation) - this was the only variant that significantly attenuated the bias in the closest available analogue (contusions in TBI) [12].
  7. For WMH volume itself, report at least one cross-check: WMH-SynthSeg on T1 (good T1-only performance in stroke [23]) or SAMSEG (multi-site-robust [22][35]) alongside the FSL/BIANCA-based numbers, and state which is the primary measure.

Trägt

Every reference was retrieved in the task; the tool quellen-pruefen found 0 wrong PMIDs/titles and all DOIs registered (2026-10-17); each reference has a one-sentence "Shows" summary from its abstract; claims not supported by the cited abstract are marked (unverified).

Trägt nicht

Abstract-level evidence only (no full texts read); a literature map, not a systematic review. Open questions:

  • No retrieved study validates the exact combination "mirror-filled infarct plus FSL lesion_filling for WMH, with 1 ml / 10 ml thresholds" in a stroke cohort; our pipeline is assembled from method papers, not validated as a whole (unverified).
  • Threshold effects (1 ml / 10 ml) are not studied anywhere in the retrieved literature and are unverified as a bias-relevant cut-off.
  • Filling versus not filling is largely a wash for whole-brain volume in MS across six packages [8], but stroke lesions (cavities, cysts, CSF-like intensity) behave differently from MS WM lesions; comparable stroke-specific validation on whole-brain/GM volumes is missing.
  • Artefact burden of FSL lesion_filling at high WMH load is documented for network analysis [33] but not quantified for volumetry.
  • Long-term longitudinal stability of deep-learning inpainting (FastSurfer-LIT, MSRepaint) across scanners and over years is not yet established [18][20][21].
  • Reporting standards: there is no consensus on which version (filled / unfilled / masked) should be published as the primary analysis in stroke cohorts [20].

Bruchindikator

A cited abstract that does not contain the claim, or a newer study that contradicts the recommendation.

Ablesung

All references were retrieved in this task from PubMed (E-utilities) and Europe PMC; abstracts were read. Bracketed numbers are those used in the text. All entries below are PubMed-indexed journal articles.

[1] Chard DT; Jackson JS; Miller DH; et al. (2010). Reducing the impact of white matter lesions on automated measures of brain gray and white matter volumes. Journal of Magnetic Resonance Imaging. DOI:10.1002/jmri.22214. PMID:20575080. Shows: simulated white-matter lesions of 15 ml at 70% of normal WM intensity inflated SPM gray-matter fractions by about 2.3% and lowered white-matter fractions by about 3.6%, and filling the lesions with values approximating normal WM (LEAP) reduced these errors to about 0.1%; cited as the foundational demonstration that lesion filling makes tissue-class volumetry valid.

[2] Battaglini M; Jenkinson M; De Stefano N (2012). Evaluating and reducing the impact of white matter lesions on brain volume measurements. Human Brain Mapping. DOI:10.1002/hbm.21344. PMID:21882300. Shows: the lesion-induced tissue misclassification of segmentation-based SIENAX varies strongly with lesion size and intensity (worst when lesion intensity resembles the GM/WM interface) while registration-based SIENA is not biased, that masking the lesions out leaves the misclassification in place, and that refilling with normal-appearing white-matter intensities restores accurate tissue-class measurements.

[3] Gelineau-Morel R; Tomassini V; Jenkinson M; et al. (2012). The effect of hypointense white matter lesions on automated gray matter segmentation in multiple sclerosis. Human Brain Mapping. DOI:10.1002/hbm.21402. PMID:21976406. Shows: higher WM lesion volume artificially reduced total gray-matter volume in real MS data by about 72% of the simulated effect, misclassified voxels sat at the GM/WM border and could be remote from the lesion, and lesion filling corrected this and revealed lateralised deep-gray-matter shape changes.

[4] Valverde S; Oliver A; Llado X (2014). A white matter lesion-filling approach to improve brain tissue volume measurements. NeuroImage: Clinical. DOI:10.1016/j.nicl.2014.08.016. PMID:25379419. Shows: refilling lesion voxels with random values drawn from a per-2D-slice white-matter intensity distribution yielded the lowest deviation in WM volume at 1.5 T and performed at least as well as state-of-the-art refilling at 3 T, i.e. simple intensity-matched filling is competitive.

[5] Prados F; Cardoso MJ; Kanber B; et al. (2016). A multi-time-point modality-agnostic patch-based method for lesion filling in multiple sclerosis. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2016.06.053. PMID:27377222. Conference precursor: Prados F; et al. (2014), MICCAI, DOI:10.1007/978-3-319-10470-6_97, PMID:25485451. Shows: a 5D patch-based multi-modality, multi-time-point non-local-means algorithm fills lesions with the most plausible texture and introduces less bias, fewer artefacts and fewer spurious edges than existing filling techniques, even when lesions neighbour gray matter or CSF; cited as the reference patch-based inpainting method.

[6] Magon S; Gaetano L; Chakravarty MM; et al. (2014). White matter lesion filling improves the accuracy of cortical thickness measurements in multiple sclerosis patients: a longitudinal study. BMC Neuroscience. DOI:10.1186/1471-2202-15-106. PMID:25200127. Shows: on unfilled T1 images 72% of WM lesion volume was misclassified as gray matter, filling raised correct lesion classification to 94%, mean cortical thickness was significantly thinner on unfilled images, and vertex-wise longitudinal analyses were more sensitive after filling.

[7] Guo C; Ferreira D; Fink K; et al. (2019). Repeatability and reproducibility of FreeSurfer, FSL-SIENAX and SPM brain volumetric measurements and the effect of lesion filling in multiple sclerosis. European Radiology. DOI:10.1007/s00330-018-5710-x. PMID:30242503. Shows: MS lesion filling changed the output of FSL-SIENAX, SPM and SPM-CAT but not of FreeSurfer, with within-scanner CoV of 0.17-0.96% against 0.65-5.0% between scanners, and intracranial-volume normalisation improved inter-scanner reproducibility; cited because the filling effect is software-specific and the scanner must stay constant.

[8] van Nederpelt DR; Amiri H; Brouwer I; et al. (2023). Reliability of brain atrophy measurements in multiple sclerosis using MRI: an assessment of six freely available software packages for cross-sectional analyses. Neuroradiology. DOI:10.1007/s00234-023-03189-8. PMID:37526657. Shows: across FreeSurfer, FSL, SAMSEG, FastSurfer, CAT-12 and SynthSeg in 21 MS patients scanned on three 3 T scanners there were no clear differences between lesion-filled and non-filled images, while between-scanner differences dominated (smallest detectable change 4.83% for SynthSeg thalamus up to 29.25%).

[9] de Sitter A; Verhoeven T; Burggraaff J; et al. (2020). Reduced accuracy of MRI deep grey matter segmentation in multiple sclerosis: an evaluation of four automated methods against manual reference segmentations in a multi-center cohort. Journal of Neurology. DOI:10.1007/s00415-020-10023-1. PMID:32621103. Shows: automated deep-gray-matter segmentation (FSL-FIRST, FreeSurfer, Geodesic Information Flow, volBrain) was significantly less accurate in 21 MS patients than in 11 controls, the accuracy degraded with lesion volume and atrophy, and lesion filling did not substantially change the segmentations.

[10] Clerigues A; Valverde S; Salvi J; et al. (2023). Minimizing the effect of white matter lesions on deep learning based tissue segmentation for brain volumetry. Computerized Medical Imaging and Graphics. DOI:10.1016/j.compmedimag.2022.102157. PMID:36535217. Shows: a 3D patch-based deep-learning framework performing end-to-end inpainting and segmentation produced a significantly smaller and more localised error from artificial WM lesions than a classical reference segmentation method, which showed much larger global differences.

[11] Dadar M; Potvin O; Camicioli R; et al. (2021). Beware of white matter hyperintensities causing systematic errors in FreeSurfer gray matter segmentations! Human Brain Mapping. DOI:10.1002/hbm.25398. PMID:33783933. Shows: in 1,077 ADNI participants WMH overlapped subcortical gray-matter volumes and biased FreeSurfer estimates, so that uncorrected caudate volume increased with age and did not separate AD patients from controls, while WMH-corrected volumes decreased with age, distinguished AD and correlated with cognition; cited as the argument that WMH must be corrected rather than ignored.

[12] Brennan D; Schneider ALC; Shinohara RT; et al. (2026). Contusions bias cortical thickness estimates after traumatic brain injury: A TRACK-TBI study. NeuroImage: Clinical. DOI:10.1016/j.nicl.2026.104003. PMID:42114207. Shows: in 86 TRACK-TBI participants contusions inflated cross-sectional cortical thickness and biased longitudinal thickness change, the default and summarisation-only masking strategies stayed biased, and masking lesion voxels from thickness estimation significantly attenuated the bias.

[13] Billot B; Greve DN; Puonti O; et al. (2023). SynthSeg: Segmentation of brain MRI scans of any contrast and resolution without retraining. Medical Image Analysis. DOI:10.1016/j.media.2023.102789. PMID:36857946. Shows: SynthSeg, trained on synthetic data with fully randomised contrast and resolution, segments real scans of six modalities and ten resolutions without retraining or fine-tuning and generalises better than supervised CNNs and Bayesian segmentation; cited as the contrast- and resolution-robust segmentation route.

[14] Cerri S; Puonti O; Meier DS; et al. (2021). A contrast-adaptive method for simultaneous whole-brain and lesion segmentation in multiple sclerosis. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.117471. PMID:33099007. Shows: a contrast-adaptive generative model with an explicit white-matter lesion model segments MS lesions and dozens of normal-appearing structures simultaneously across four datasets without retraining, also works on healthy controls, and is released inside FreeSurfer (SAMSEG).

[15] Puonti O; Iglesias JE; Van Leemput K (2016). Fast and sequence-adaptive whole-brain segmentation using parametric Bayesian modeling. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2016.09.011. PMID:27612647. Shows: a generative parametric Bayesian whole-brain segmentation method matched state-of-the-art accuracy on T1-weighted scans while being one to two orders of magnitude faster and robust to different contrast, multi-contrast data and small training sets; cited as the model underlying SAMSEG.

[16] Sederevicius D; Vidal-Pineiro D; Sorensen O; et al. (2021). Reliability and sensitivity of two whole-brain segmentation approaches included in FreeSurfer - ASEG and SAMSEG. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118113. PMID:33940143. Shows: both ASEG and SAMSEG are reliable and detect within-person longitudinal change, but their age trajectories differ for most structures while SAMSEG yields significantly lower differences between repeated measures; cited as the reason not to mix engines within one longitudinal study.

[17] Radwan AM; Emsell L; Blommaert J; et al. (2021). Virtual brain grafting: Enabling whole brain parcellation in the presence of large lesions. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.117731. PMID:33454411. Shows: Virtual Brain Grafting generates a lesion-free T1 and parcellates brains with large, bilateral, heterogeneous lesions better than a non-VBG approach (synthetic Dice 0.894 unilateral and 0.903 bilateral versus 0.617), with neuroradiologist assessment confirming the improvement.

[18] Pollak C; Kuegler D; Bauer T; et al. (2025). FastSurfer-LIT: Lesion inpainting tool for whole-brain MRI segmentation with tumors, cavities, and abnormalities. Imaging Neuroscience. DOI:10.1162/imag_a_00446. PMID:40109899. Shows: FastSurfer-LIT, a denoising-diffusion inpainting tool, outperformed previously proposed solutions on simulated tumours (N = 100) and synthetic MS lesions (N = 39) with better Dice and Hausdorff measures, and increased the reliability of reproducing pre-operative cortical thickness estimates after resection surgery.

[19] Billot B; Magdamo C; Cheng Y; et al. (2023). Robust machine learning segmentation for large-scale analysis of heterogeneous clinical brain MRI datasets. PNAS. DOI:10.1073/pnas.2216399120. PMID:36802420. Shows: SynthSeg+ adds cortical parcellation, intracranial-volume estimation and automated detection of faulty segmentations to label-only whole-brain segmentation and accurately replicates atrophy patterns on 14,000 heterogeneous clinical scans.

[20] Deutscher E; Imms P; Irimia A; et al. (2026). The evolution of T1-weighted lesion inpainting tools in patients with brain injury: A scoping review. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2026.121869. PMID:41881175. Shows: a scoping review of 24 inpainting papers for acquired brain injury classifies the tools into traditional (local/global diffusion, search- and a-priori patch-based, low-rank sparse) and deep-learning (CNN, GAN, diffusion) families, discusses their strengths and limitations, and recommends steps to increase the adoption of lesion inpainting; cited as the map of the available tools.

[21] Zhang J; Zuo L; Liu Y; et al. (2026). MSRepaint: Multiple Sclerosis Repaint with conditional denoising diffusion implicit model for bidirectional lesion filling and synthesis. Medical Image Analysis. DOI:10.1016/j.media.2026.104338. PMID:42826585. Shows: MSRepaint, a mask-conditioned diffusion model for bidirectional lesion filling and synthesis, outperformed the FSL and NiftySeg filling methods and reached the accuracy of FastSurfer-LIT with over 20x faster inference, while training MS lesion segmentation models on its synthetic lesions beat CarveMix-synthesised and real ISBI data; cited as the current deep-learning benchmark for lesion filling.

[22] Ferris JK; Tavenner BP; Khlif MS; et al. (2023). Optimizing automated white matter hyperintensity segmentation in individuals with stroke. Frontiers in Neuroimaging. DOI:10.3389/fnimg.2023.1099301. PMID:37554631. Shows: an optimised BIANCA protocol performed well within a dataset but failed when a trained model was applied to a new dataset, whereas the unsupervised SAMSEG needed no WMH masks, was more robust to multi-site data and was only slightly worse than BIANCA within a single site; cited as the stroke-specific validation of both tools.

[23] Khan MH; Chakraborty S; Ferris JK; et al. (2026). Estimating white matter hyperintensities volume in individuals with stroke using T1-weighted images. NeuroImage: Reports. DOI:10.1016/j.ynirp.2026.100344. PMID:42011418. Preprint: medRxiv (2025), DOI:10.1101/2025.10.22.25338564, PMID:41282712. Shows: in 227 stroke patients WMH-SynthSeg with T1-only input produced more accurate and reliable WMH volumes than SAMSEG with T1-only and performed comparably to SAMSEG with T1 plus FLAIR; cited as evidence that WMH can be quantified from T1 alone when FLAIR is unavailable.

[24] Gaubert M; Dell'Orco A; Lange C; et al. (2022). Performance evaluation of automated white matter hyperintensity segmentation algorithms in a multicenter cohort on cognitive impairment and dementia. Frontiers in Psychiatry. DOI:10.3389/fpsyt.2022.1010273. PMID:36713907. Shows: in 50 DELCODE FLAIR scans the retrained deep-learning algorithm sysu_media achieved the best performance (average Dice 0.702, F1 0.642), intra-class correlation was excellent for all algorithms except BIANCA (0.835), and performance improved with higher WMH burden; cited as the multicentre benchmark of WMH segmentation tools.

[25] Torres-Simon L; Del Cerro-Leon A; Yus M; et al. (2024). Decoding the best automated segmentation tools for vascular white matter hyperintensities in the aging brain: a clinician's guide to precision and purpose. GeroScience. DOI:10.1007/s11357-024-01238-5. PMID:38869712. Shows: against clinician manual segmentations the supervised algorithms LST-LPA and BIANCA were superior, particularly for small WMH, and their consistency improved when run in parallel with the unsupervised LST-LGA and SAMSEG, with the resulting threshold identifying at-risk individuals in a cohort of 300; cited as guidance on which WMH tool fits which purpose.

[26] Schmidt P; Gaser C; Arsic M; et al. (2012). An automated tool for detection of FLAIR-hyperintense white-matter lesions in multiple sclerosis. NeuroImage. DOI:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2011.11.032. PMID:22119648. Shows: an automated algorithm requiring a 3D gradient-echo T1-weighted plus a FLAIR image at 3 T detected FLAIR-hyperintense MS lesions in 53 patients, 10 patients with posterior fossa lesions and 18 controls with R2 above 0.93 against manual tracing; cited as the origin of the LST lesion-segmentation family.

[27] Wiltgen T; McGinnis J; Schlaeger S; et al. (2024). LST-AI: A deep learning ensemble for accurate MS lesion segmentation. NeuroImage: Clinical. DOI:10.1016/j.nicl.2024.103611. PMID:38703470. Shows: LST-AI, an openly available deep-learning ensemble of three 3D U-Nets trained on 491 MS T1/FLAIR image pairs, exceeded Dice and F1 scores of 0.62 on 103 test cases while LST, SAMSEG and nnU-Net stayed below 0.56, and it also includes lesion location annotation; cited as the state of the art for MS lesion segmentation.

[28] Ling Y; Jouvent E; Cousyn L; et al. (2018). Validation and optimization of BIANCA for the segmentation of extensive white matter hyperintensities. Neuroinformatics. DOI:10.1007/s12021-018-9372-2. PMID:29594711. Shows: in patients with extensive WMH the best BIANCA configurations were multimodal FLAIR plus T1 input, a mixed lesion-load training set and an individually chosen threshold (median Dice 0.80, ICC 0.97), though the difference from the reference grew linearly with total WMH burden; cited as the BIANCA setup reference.

[29] Deutscher E; Dennis E; Hillary FG; et al. (2025). FastSurfer parcellation accuracy after lesion filling in moderate-to-severe traumatic brain injury. Frontiers in Neurology. DOI:10.3389/fneur.2025.1652385. PMID:41451415. Shows: in 140 synthetically lesioned ms-TBI images FastSurfer parcellations of the lesioned images were significantly more accurate (DSC 0.93, PVD -0.40) than those of the virtual-brain-grafted images (DSC 0.81, PVD -9.03), which the authors read as FastSurfer robustness to such focal lesions; cited as the cautionary counterexample that filling can hurt.

[30] Khlif MS; Egorova N; Werden E; et al. (2019). A comparison of automated segmentation and manual tracing in estimating hippocampal volume in ischemic stroke and healthy control participants. NeuroImage: Clinical. DOI:10.1016/j.nicl.2018.10.019. PMID:30606656. Shows: among eight automated hippocampal segmentation methods in 105 ischemic stroke patients and 39 controls FreeSurfer-Subfields scored highest (96.3%), and masking the stroke lesions before segmentation made the hippocampal volumes agree less with manual tracing, so the authors recommend automated segmentation without lesion masking in stroke; cited against blanket lesion masking.

[31] Miller GN; Steeby CJ; Ortega-Marquez J; et al. (2025). Comparison of brain normalization software and lesion compensation techniques in chronic perinatal stroke imaging. Imaging Neuroscience. DOI:10.1162/IMAG.a.1048. PMID:42344990. Shows: registering eleven variably distorted perinatal stroke brains showed ANTs and EasyReg to be significantly more accurate than FNIRT (all three struggled with large lesions), and inserting a healthy tissue mask before registration significantly improved FNIRT's lesion-mask normalisation; cited because lesion compensation matters for registration in stroke.

[32] Deutscher E; Dennis E; Burnett J; et al. (2025). ENIGMA's advanced guide for parcellation error identification (EAGLE-I): An implementation in the context of brain lesions. MethodsX. DOI:10.1016/j.mex.2025.103482. PMID:40791643. Shows: EAGLE-I is a training resource that classifies parcellation region errors by type (unconnected versus connected), size and directionality and records them on a standardised spreadsheet that automatically computes a brain quality rating (pass to fail); cited as the dedicated QC guide for lesioned brains.

[33] van der Weijden CWJ; Pitombeira MS; Haveman YRA; et al. (2022). The effect of lesion filling on brain network analysis in multiple sclerosis using structural magnetic resonance imaging. Insights into Imaging. DOI:10.1186/s13244-022-01198-4. PMID:35347460. Shows: in 49 MS patients and 19 controls, artefacts in the lesion-filled T1 images correlated positively with total lesion volume (r = 0.84) and had a major impact on gray-matter segmentation accuracy, while filling also increased the detection rate of network alterations; cited as the reason to apply filling cautiously at high lesion load.

[34] Busby N; Kristinsson S; Johnson L; et al. (2025). White matter hyperintensity load independent from the stroke lesion is associated with chronic aphasia severity and treatment outcome. Stroke. DOI:10.1161/STROKEAHA.124.046710. PMID:40276856. Shows: in 95 people with chronic post-stroke aphasia whole-brain Fazekas ratings were consistently higher than right-hemisphere ratings, right-hemisphere ratings correlated more strongly with language behaviour, and only right-hemisphere periventricular WMH was associated with naming improvement after therapy; cited as the argument to measure WMH contralateral to the infarct.

[35] Ferris JK; Lo BP; Barisano G; et al. (2024). Modulation of the association between corticospinal tract damage and outcome after stroke by white matter hyperintensities. Neurology. DOI:10.1212/WNL.0000000000209387. PMID:38701386. Shows: in 223 participants of the ENIGMA Stroke Recovery Working Group WMH volume related to motor impairment above and beyond weighted corticospinal tract lesion load, and in moderate-to-severe WMH only WMH volume predicted impairment while CST lesion load no longer did; cited as evidence that WMH carry prognostic information independent of the stroke lesion.

[36] Hotz I; Deschwanden PF; Liem F; et al. (2022). Performance of three freely available methods for extracting white matter hyperintensities: FreeSurfer, UBO Detector, and BIANCA. Human Brain Mapping. DOI:10.1002/hbm.25739. PMID:34873789. Shows: in 231 cognitively healthy older adults FreeSurfer (T1w) underestimated WMH volume and had the worst Dice (0.434), BIANCA achieved the highest Dice in 3D FLAIR (0.602) but only a moderate relation to Fazekas scores, and UBO Detector matched BIANCA and had the strongest Fazekas association in 2D FLAIR; cited as the comparison of freely available WMH extraction methods.

[37] Sdika M; Pelletier D (2009). Nonrigid registration of multiple sclerosis brain images using lesion inpainting for morphometry or lesion mapping. Human Brain Mapping. DOI:10.1002/hbm.20566. PMID:18412131. Shows: MS lesions distort non-rigid registration, and filling the lesions so the brain resembles a healthy brain before registration removed this bias, with the proposed filling method outperforming the state of the art; cited as the first inpainting-for-registration application.

[38] Solodkin A; Hasson U; Siugzdaite R; et al. (2010). Virtual brain transplantation (VBT): a method for accurate image registration and parcellation in large cortical stroke. Archives Italiennes de Biologie. PMID:21175010. (No DOI in the PubMed record.) Shows: in thirteen chronic patients with a single large left-hemisphere stroke, filling the lesion with virtual tissue transplanted from the non-stroke hemisphere yielded FreeSurfer parcellations of twelve regions that did not differ between the lesioned and the non-lesioned hemisphere; cited as the lineage of mirror-based infarct filling.

[39] Hannoun S; Fayad G; El Ayoubi NK; et al. (2025). The effect of lesion filling on brain age estimation in multiple sclerosis. BMC Medical Imaging. DOI:10.1186/s12880-025-01897-6. PMID:40866833. Shows: in 571 relapsing-remitting MS patients brain age estimates from lesion-filled and non-filled T1 images agreed almost perfectly (r = 0.97, ICC = 0.962, mean difference 1.23 years) and lesion filling only modestly improved the structural interpretability of the brain age gap; cited as evidence that filling has little effect on this endpoint.

Claims not supported by the abstracts

Three distinct claims from sections 2-4 are marked "(unverified)" in the text (four marker sites, because the SynthSeg/SynthSeg+ negative claim is stated twice), because the abstract of the reference(s) they cite does not establish them. All remaining claims in sections 2-4 were checked sentence by sentence against the retrieved abstracts and are supported.

  • Sections 2 and 3.2: "the FSL/LST family is the standard building block in clinical pipelines [2][7][26]". Reference [1] supports the ~2.3% / ~3.6% -> ~0.1% error reduction, but none of [2][7][26] states that this family is the standard building block; the abstracts only show that these tools use it ([2][7][26] use FSL/LST filling, they do not compare adoption across pipelines).
  • Section 3.2: "Random-intensity filling ... performed at least as well as state-of-the-art refilling (lowest WM deviation for FAST/SPM8 at 1.5 T and 3 T) [4]". The [4] abstract reports the lowest WM deviation at 1.5 T for both segmenters and at 3 T only for FAST; with SPM8 at 3 T the method merely equalled the best method, so the "at 1.5 T and 3 T" phrasing overstates the abstract.
  • Sections 2 and 3.5: "Neither [SynthSeg nor SynthSeg+] models lesion tissue as a class [13][19]". This is a negative claim: the [13][19] abstracts describe no lesion class, but absence from an abstract is not evidence of absence in the model, so it is not verifiable from the abstracts alone. (This marker was already present in the report before the check; it is listed here for completeness.)

Not listed: the two statements already flagged as "(unverified)" by the report itself in section 5 (no study validates our exact mirror + FSL lesion_filling + 1 ml / 10 ml combination; the 1 ml / 10 ml thresholds are not studied as a bias-relevant cut-off) are explicit statements about missing literature rather than claims that cite a reference as support.
