Herkunft: research report by the remote-model agent (Clara on the hospital model hub) with PubMed/Europe PMC/arXiv retrieval, checked by Claude Code 2026-10-17 (quellen-pruefen 0 wrong, 10/10 'Shows' spot checks against abstracts) · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  • FreeSurfer aseg, SynthSeg/SynthSeg+, FastSurfer, SAMSEG and FSL FIRST are all usable for the large structures (whole hippocampus, striatum, thalamus, brainstem); the choice is decided less by peak accuracy than by longitudinal use, lesion handling and site harmonisation [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,10].
  • For a longitudinal cohort the decisive step is processing all sessions through one unbiased within-subject template (FreeSurfer longitudinal stream) or an equivalent longitudinal registration; treating sessions independently injects processing variance into the change signal [7,27].
  • Lesion filling of T1 is sensible before any T1-based segmentation, but in the one retrieved head-to-head test lesion filling did not change between-scanner and within-scanner agreement for the six tested tools; the residual scanner effect was the larger problem [6].
  • Reported smallest detectable change (SDC) is small within a scanner (1.0% for SAMSEG whole brain) but much larger between scanners (4.8% for SynthSeg thalamus up to 29.3% for CAT12 thalamus), which is the real limit on detecting atrophy [6].
  • Accuracy checks against manual tracing show automated packages systematically overestimate basal-ganglia and thalamic volumes; the pallidum and thalamus are the weakest structures, and errors are worst for the smallest volumes [4].
  • Hippocampal subfields and amygdalar nuclei are only reliable above roughly 200–300 mm3 per structure (unverified); small nuclei such as fimbria and hippocampal fissure have ~15% reproducibility error [11,12].
  • Thalamic nuclei need a dedicated atlas (optionally with diffusion MRI); they are not a standard output of the general packages [13,14].
  • Harmonisation is not optional: all retrieved harmonisation studies show large site or scanner effects in volumetric and cortical measures, and ComBat/longComBat remove them while preserving biological variance; longComBat is more powerful for longitudinal change than cross-sectional ComBat [15,16,17,18].
  • Automatic QC flags exist (SynthSeg+ faulty-segmentation detection, MRIQC/Qoala-T style outlier metrics), but manual inspection of a sample remains necessary; manual editing of surfaces changes subcortical volumes substantially [3,10,19].
  • The specific claim that SynthSeg underestimates hippocampal atrophy is unverified in the retrieved literature; plausible mechanisms below, but it must be tested in-house against the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream before it is accepted as fact [6,11,12].

Practical recommendation (as written for our setup)

Given the retrieved evidence and the existing SynthSeg + FreeSurfer 8.x local setup, I recommend a two-track pipeline with a single primary analysis track and a cross-check.

Primary track (volumetric readout for group statistics over time): 1. Build one unbiased within-subject template per patient from all available sessions and process every session against it (FreeSurfer longitudinal stream, or an equivalent unbiased longitudinal registration). This is the step that most directly reduces the processing variance that currently mixes into the change signal [7,30]. 2. Use lesion-filled T1 for segmentation, but do not expect lesion filling to remove scanner effects; it addresses lesion-driven, not site-driven, variance [6,23]. Keep the FLAIR-based lesion masks under a validated segmenter for the lesion maps themselves [32]. 3. Keep SAMSEG (lesion-aware variant) as the primary T1 segmenter for the basal ganglia, thalamus and brainstem. Justification: it was the most repeatable within scanner in the 3 T multicentre test (whole brain SDC 1.02%), is sequence-adaptive and protocol-robust, and has a lesion-aware mode (unverified); it also delivered among the best between-scanner SDC for thalamus with SynthSeg [6,8,9]. 4. Keep the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream (or FastSurfer-long) in parallel as the reference segmentation for the same structures, because it is the best-validated longitudinal framework and enables the hippocampal-subfield branch [7,12,27]. 5. Derive harmonisation from the raw volumes: apply longitudinal ComBat with scanner/ site as batch and age/interval as covariates, and fit it on a training split. Cross-sectional ComBat is an acceptable fallback, but expect lower power for change; and if ComBat is fitted without training data it may fail to recover atrophy, so always check that harmonisation preserves the expected atrophy contrast [17,18]. 6. Report structures only where the retrieved reliability evidence supports them: whole hippocampus, striatum, thalamus, brainstem and larger amygdala volumes are the robust core; hippocampal subfields above ~300 mm3 and amygdalar nuclei above ~200 mm3 only if the cohort-specific test-retest supports them; fimbria and hippocampal fissure should not be primary endpoints [4,11,12,27]. 7. Do not use thalamic nuclei as a primary endpoint in this cohort unless diffusion MRI is available and a nuclei-specific atlas is deployed; they are not a standard output of the general tools and their reliability thresholds are stricter still [13,14].

Cross-check track (this directly addresses the observed underestimation): 8. On 20–30 patients, run FreeSurfer-long, SAMSEG and SynthSeg side by side on the same lesion-filled, template-registered T1s and compare the annualised percentage change of hippocampal volume, not the cross-sectional volumes. Report the tool-specific SDC and compute whether each tool can separate patients with expected decline from stable patients. Use the within-scanner versus between-scanner SDC decomposition from [6] as the template for this analysis. 9. Treat the "SynthSeg underestimates hippocampal atrophy" statement as a hypothesis to test, because no retrieved abstract states it. The retrieved facts that make it plausible are: SynthSeg has no learned spatial prior (unverified), so it cannot regularise a partly lesioned or low-contrast anterior hippocampus [2]; most hippocampus methods over-segment at the anterior border and degrade on clinical data [11]; and hippocampal-subfield reliability is strongly volume-dependent, so small structures carry the largest error [12,27]. 10. Regardless of the chosen segmenter, run automatic QC (SynthSeg+ faulty-segmentation flag plus an MRIQC/Qoala-T-style outlier pass on global measures, Euler number and CNR) and manually review the flagged scans; a semi-automated strategy was the best-performing one in the retrieved QC comparison (unverified), and manual editing moves subcortical volumes substantially [3,19].

If the group is willing to invest in task-specific training, a fine-tuned nnU-Net (or an ensemble) on locally traced stroke data is the most promising route to a single lesion-robust segmenter, based on the nnU-Net benchmark and the successful task-specific brainstem and single-contrast MS models [21,23,24]; a local training set of even a few dozen traced cases is a realistic starting point.

Trägt

Every reference was retrieved in the task; the tool quellen-pruefen found 0 wrong PMIDs/titles and all DOIs registered (2026-10-17); each reference has a one-sentence "Shows" summary from its abstract; claims not supported by the cited abstract are marked (unverified).

Trägt nicht

Abstract-level evidence only (no full texts read); a literature map, not a systematic review. Open questions:

  • No retrieved study measured hippocampal atrophy rate with SynthSeg against the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream in a stroke cohort; the head-to-head evidence is from MS and from cross-sectional accuracy benchmarks. This gap is the core of the local observation and should be closed with the cross-check track in section 4.
  • The retrieved multicentre test found no clear effect of lesion filling on agreement [6], which conflicts with the intuitive expectation and with the lesion-aware brainstem result [23]; a stroke-specific replication is missing.
  • WMH effects on segmentation are documented but the best correction method is explicitly left open by the retrieved paper [22].
  • Ventricle enlargement is not separately quantified in the retrieved abstracts; its interaction with basal-ganglia and thalamic segmentation in stroke is unverified from this evidence base.
  • Between-scanner SDC (4.8–29.3%) [6] and the harmonisation results [17,18] together imply that a 5-year, 4-session, multi-site design may need within-site-only change estimates or a very carefully fitted longitudinal ComBat; the required sample size for a given effect size was not retrieved and is unverified.
  • Thalamic nuclei and hippocampal subfield reliability in the presence of basal-ganglia infarcts specifically was not found in the retrieved literature.

Bruchindikator

A cited abstract that does not contain the claim, or a newer study that contradicts the recommendation.

Ablesung

All entries were retrieved in this task via PubMed E-utilities / Europe PMC REST; DOIs and PMIDs are the ones the API returned for each record. Authors, year, title, journal and DOI were re-extracted programmatically from the stored XML (REFS_VERIFIED.txt in this folder). No arXiv source was used. No reference here was written from memory.

[1] Fischl B, Salat DH, Busa E, et al. (2002). Whole brain segmentation: automated labeling of neuroanatomical structures in the human brain. Neuron. doi:10.1016/s0896-6273(02)00569-x. PMID:11832223. Shows: In this FreeSurfer aseg method, voxels are labelled with one of 37 classes (including caudate, putamen, pallidum, thalamus, hippocampus and amygdala) from probabilistic information of a manually labelled training set, with accuracy comparable to manual labelling and robustness to anatomical variability such as ventricular enlargement - it is the baseline segmentation of our FreeSurfer branch.

[2] Billot B, Greve DN, Puonti O, et al. (2023). SynthSeg: Segmentation of brain MRI scans of any contrast and resolution without retraining. Med Image Anal. doi:10.1016/j.media.2023.102789. PMID:36857946. Shows: A CNN trained only on synthetic scans with fully randomised contrast and resolution segments real scans of six modalities and ten resolutions without retraining or fine-tuning, which is the cross-contrast robustness claimed for the local SynthSeg setup.

[3] Billot B, Magdamo C, Cheng Y, et al. (2023). Robust machine learning segmentation for large-scale analysis of heterogeneous clinical brain MRI datasets. Proc Natl Acad Sci U S A. doi:10.1073/pnas.2216399120. PMID:36802420. Shows: SynthSeg+ adds cortical parcellation, intracranial volume estimation and automated detection of faulty segmentations to whole-brain segmentation and replicated atrophy patterns in an ageing study of 14,000 scans - cited as an automatic QC flag for a large cohort.

[4] Makowski C, Beland S, Kostopoulos P, et al. (2018). Evaluating accuracy of striatal, pallidal, and thalamic segmentation methods: Comparing automated approaches to manual delineation. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2017.02.069. PMID:28259781. Shows: Against manual delineation of striatum, globus pallidus and thalamus in 30 subjects all three automated packages (FreeSurfer, FSL, MAGeT-Brain) overestimated volumes, the discrepancies were most pronounced for globus pallidus and thalamus and largest at the lower end of the volume distribution - the accuracy caveat for basal ganglia and thalamus.

[5] Henschel L, Conjeti S, Estrada S, et al. (2020). FastSurfer - A fast and accurate deep learning based neuroimaging pipeline. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.117012. PMID:32526386. Shows: FastSurfer replicates FreeSurfer's anatomical segmentation (95 classes) including surface reconstruction and cortical parcellation as a deep-learning pipeline, with high accuracy on unseen data and increased test-retest reliability, in a fraction of FreeSurfer's runtime - cited as the scalable FreeSurfer alternative.

[6] van Nederpelt DR, Amiri H, Brouwer I, et al. (2023). Reliability of brain atrophy measurements in multiple sclerosis using MRI: an assessment of six freely available software packages for cross-sectional analyses. Neuroradiology. doi:10.1007/s00234-023-03189-8. PMID:37526657. Shows: In 21 people with MS rescanned on three 3 T scanners, all six packages reached good to excellent agreement for large structures, lesion filling made no clear difference and between-scanner smallest detectable change was large (4.83% for SynthSeg thalamus up to 29.25% for CAT12 thalamus), which hampers clinical use of atrophy measurements - the source of the SDC figures used here.

[7] Reuter M, Schmansky NJ, Rosas HD, et al. (2012). Within-subject template estimation for unbiased longitudinal image analysis. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2012.02.084. PMID:22430496. Shows: An unbiased, robust within-subject template built from all time points and identical processing of every time point reduce processing bias and over-regularisation and increase precision and discrimination power - the justification for the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream.

[8] Puonti O, Iglesias JE, Van Leemput K (2016). Fast and sequence-adaptive whole-brain segmentation using parametric Bayesian modeling. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2016.09.011. PMID:27612647. Shows: A sequence-adaptive parametric Bayesian whole-brain segmentation reached state-of-the-art accuracy on four datasets with different scanners, field strengths and sequences while being one to two orders of magnitude faster, and is robust to small training sets - cited for SAMSEG.

[9] Billot B, Cerri S, Van Leemput K, et al. (2021). Joint segmentation of multiple sclerosis lesions and brain anatomy in MRI scans of any contrast and resolution with CNNs. Proc IEEE Int Symp Biomed Imaging. doi:10.1109/isbi48211.2021.9434127. PMID:34367472. Shows: A CNN that trains only on synthetic scans with simulated lesions segments MS lesions and brain anatomy jointly in scans of any contrast and resolution and was compared with a state-of-the-art Bayesian FreeSurfer approach and with dataset-specific CNNs trained on real data - cited here for lesion-aware segmentation, although the abstract describes a SynthSeg-based CNN and not an extension of SAMSEG (see section 7).

[10] Dewey J, Hana G, Russell T, et al. (2010). Reliability and validity of MRI-based automated volumetry software relative to auto-assisted manual measurement of subcortical structures in HIV-infected patients from a multisite study. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2010.03.033. PMID:20338250. Shows: Against auto-assisted manual tracing of caudate, putamen, amygdala and hippocampus in 120 HIV patients of a multisite study, FreeSurfer showed the lower absolute volume difference in five structures, higher sensitivity in seven and higher spatial overlap in all eight, with less variability than IBASPM, yet the authors still require visual inspection and manual correction.

[11] Sghirripa S, Bhalerao G, Griffanti L, et al. (2025). Evaluating Traditional, Deep Learning and Subfield Methods for Automatically Segmenting the Hippocampus From MRI. Hum Brain Mapp. doi:10.1002/hbm.70200. PMID:40143669. Shows: A benchmark of ten automatic hippocampus methods found that most, especially deep-learning methods trained on manual labels, performed well on public data but showed more error and variability on clinical data and often over-segmented the anterior hippocampus - the reason to validate on a local, lesion-containing sample.

[12] Marizzoni M, Antelmi L, Bosch B, et al. (2016). Longitudinal reproducibility of automatically segmented hippocampal subfields: A multisite European 3T study on healthy elderly. Hum Brain Mapp. doi:10.1002/hbm.22859. PMID:26043939. Shows: In 65 healthy elderly scanned at 13 3 T sites, hippocampal-subfield reproducibility was very good for whole hippocampus, CA2-3, CA4-DG and subiculum (~2% error, DICE > 0.90), good for CA1 and presubiculum (~5%) and poor for fimbria and hippocampal fissure (~15%), and within-session averaging of two T1 images improved subfield but not whole-hippocampus reproducibility - the error budget of the subfield branch.

[13] Iglesias JE, Insausti R, Lerma-Usabiaga G, et al. (2018). A probabilistic atlas of the human thalamic nuclei combining ex vivo MRI and histology. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2018.08.012. PMID:30121337. Shows: A probabilistic atlas of 26 thalamic nuclei built from ex vivo MRI and serial histology of 12 thalami (12 whole thalami from six autopsy samples) segmented in vivo with excellent test-retest reliability, robustness to contrast changes and sensitivity to differential thalamic effects in Alzheimer's disease - the reference atlas for thalamic nuclei.

[14] Tregidgo HFJ, Soskic S, Althonayan J, et al. (2023). Accurate Bayesian segmentation of thalamic nuclei using diffusion MRI and an improved histological atlas. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2023.120129. PMID:37088323. Shows: A Bayesian thalamic segmentation that fuses a histological atlas with structural and diffusion MRI likelihoods labelled 25 thalamic labels per hemisphere, improving nucleus identification by up to 10 percentage points over structural MRI alone with median test-retest Dice above 0.85 - the dedicated thalamic option, but it needs diffusion MRI.

[15] Fortin JP, Parker D, Tunc B, et al. (2017). Harmonization of multi-site diffusion tensor imaging data. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2017.08.047. PMID:28826946. Shows: In FA and MD maps of 205 healthy participants, DTI measurements were highly site-specific and combining sites without harmonisation could be counter-productive, while ComBat best modelled and removed inter-site variability and preserved biological variability.

[16] Fortin JP, Cullen N, Sheline YI, et al. (2018). Harmonization of cortical thickness measurements across scanners and sites. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2017.11.024. PMID:29155184. Shows: Across two large multi-site studies and 11 scanners, ComBat removed unwanted scanner-related variance in cortical thickness values while increasing the power and reproducibility of the subsequent statistical analyses - the cross-sectional harmonisation precedent.

[17] Beer JC, Tustison NJ, Cook PA, et al. (2020). Longitudinal ComBat: A method for harmonizing longitudinal multi-scanner imaging data. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.117129. PMID:32640273. Shows: On longitudinal cortical thickness of 663 ADNI participants, longitudinal ComBat was more powerful for detecting longitudinal change than cross-sectional ComBat and controlled the type I error rate better than unharmonised data with scanner as a covariate - the recommended harmonisation for our 4-session design.

[18] Lu YC, Zuo L, Chou YY, et al. (2025). An evaluation of image-based and statistical techniques for harmonizing brain volume measurements. Imaging Neurosci (Camb). doi:10.1162/IMAG.a.73. PMID:40800765. Shows: Comparing neuroCombat, DeepHarmony and HACA3 on GRE and MPRAGE data, all improved volume consistency but HACA3 was most effective (absolute volume difference < 3%, ICC > 0.9), and neuroCombat detected simulated hippocampal atrophy only when training data were available - the caveat to fit harmonisation on a training split.

[19] Monereo-Sanchez J, de Jong JJA, Drenthen GS, et al. (2021). Quality control strategies for brain MRI segmentation and parcellation: Practical approaches and recommendations - insights from the Maastricht study. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2021.118174. PMID:34000406. Shows: Among 12 quality-control strategies applied to 259 participants of the Maastricht Study, manual QC reduced relative unexplained variance most, the best automated alternatives were based on Euler numbers and MRIQC scores, and excluding outliers on global morphological measures increased unexplained variance - the basis for our QC recommendation.

[20] Patenaude B, Smith SM, Kennedy DN, et al. (2011). A Bayesian model of shape and appearance for subcortical brain segmentation. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2011.02.046. PMID:21352927. Shows: A Bayesian shape-and-appearance model trained on 336 manually labelled T1 images segmented 15 subcortical structures with median Dice overlaps of 0.7-0.9, comparable or better than other automated methods, and is distributed as FIRST with FSL - cited as the FSL subcortical option.

[21] Isensee F, Jaeger PF, Kohl SAA, et al. (2021). nnU-Net: a self-configuring method for deep learning-based biomedical image segmentation. Nat Methods. doi:10.1038/s41592-020-01008-z. PMID:33288961. Shows: nnU-Net configures its own preprocessing, network architecture, training and post-processing and without manual intervention surpassed most existing approaches, including highly specialised ones, on 23 public datasets - the framework for fine-tuning on local stroke data.

[22] Karim HT, Andreescu C, MacCloud RL, et al. (2016). The effects of white matter disease on the accuracy of automated segmentation. Psychiatry Res Neuroimaging. doi:10.1016/j.pscychresns.2016.05.003. PMID:27254085. Shows: In a late-life depression sample, white matter hyperintensities significantly affected segmentation, whether they were filled with normal-appearing white-matter intensities or a multispectral segmentation was used, and the affected regions were not limited to the WMH themselves, so studies in high-WMH populations should account for WMH - the WMH caveat for our cohort.

[23] Gesierich B, Sander L, Pirpamer L, et al. (2025). Extended Technical and Clinical Validation of Deep Learning-Based Brainstem Segmentation for Application in Neurodegenerative Diseases. Hum Brain Mapp. doi:10.1002/hbm.70141. PMID:39936343. Shows: Two deep-learning brainstem segmentation models (MD-GRU, nnU-Net) reached median Dice >= 0.95, showed scan-rescan and inter-scanner limits of agreement similar to longitudinal FreeSurfer in patients with cerebral small vessel disease, detected brainstem atrophy in 100% of multiple system atrophy patients and improved further with lesion filling in MS - the brainstem-specific, lesion-tolerant benchmark.

[24] Goebl P, Wingrove J, Abdelmannan O, et al. (2025). Enabling new insights from old scans by repurposing clinical MRI archives for multiple sclerosis research. Nat Commun. doi:10.1038/s41467-025-58274-8. PMID:40195318. Shows: MindGlide, trained on 4,247 scans of 2,934 MS patients across 592 scanners and validated on 14,952 scans, extracted lesion and region volumes from any single MRI contrast and detected treatment effects on T2-lesion accrual and on cortical and deep grey-matter volume loss - evidence for single-contrast deep learning on archive-like data.

[25] Ozzoude M, Ramirez J, Raamana PR, et al. (2020). Cortical Thickness Estimation in Individuals With Cerebral Small Vessel Disease, Focal Atrophy, and Chronic Stroke Lesions. Front Neurosci. doi:10.3389/fnins.2020.598868. PMID:33381009. Shows: In 155 patients with cerebral small vessel disease, focal atrophy and chronic stroke lesions, a correction procedure raised acceptable QC ratings for the cortical ribbon from 18 to 76% and for tissue segmentation from 38 to 92% and reduced failures, with the corrected output only then showing the expected association with cognitive status - the evidence that lesions and atrophy need explicit correction.

[26] Schwarzer J, Khadhraoui E, Einspanner E, et al. (2025). Comparison of brain volumetry in patients with non-lesional epilepsy on 3 and 7 T MPRAGE. Quant Imaging Med Surg. doi:10.21037/qims-2025-1263. PMID:41209181. Shows: Comparing 3 T and 7 T MPRAGE in non-lesional epilepsy, FreeSurfer and FastSurfer differed significantly at both field strengths with FastSurfer giving higher volumes, and 7 T inhomogeneities caused deviations in left hippocampal sub-segmentation while 3 T hippocampus segmentation was more homogeneous - cited for the sensitivity of these tools to acquisition conditions.

[27] Quattrini G, Pievani M, Jovicich J, et al. (2020). Amygdalar nuclei and hippocampal subfields on MRI: Test-retest reliability of automated volumetry across different MRI sites and vendors. Neuroimage. doi:10.1016/j.neuroimage.2020.116932. PMID:32416226. Shows: Across two independent cohorts with repeated 3D T1 MRI at different sites and vendors, site and vendor effects were extensive for the DICE score and reliability correlated strongly with volume size: only structures above 200 mm3 (amygdalar nuclei) and 300 mm3 (hippocampal subfields, except the molecular layer) reached reproducibility error < 5% and DICE > 0.80 - the volume thresholds used for reporting.

[28] Li Q, Pardoe H, Lichter R, et al. (2015). Cortical thickness estimation in longitudinal stroke studies: A comparison of 3 measurement methods. Neuroimage Clin. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2014.08.017. PMID:26110108. Shows: Comparing one surface-based and two voxel-based cortical thickness methods in patients imaged in the first month after stroke and at 3 months, the methods differed importantly in high-curvature and peri-lesional regions but had similar reproducibility, and both approaches were judged valid in stroke - cited for the general effect of stroke lesions on automated estimates, not for subcortical volumes.

[29] Hafiz R, Farmer C, Joseph L, et al. (2026). Evaluating Precision and Responsiveness of MRI-derived Brain Volumetric and Clinical Outcome Measures in CLN3 Disease. medRxiv. doi:10.64898/2026.09.29.26363931. PMID:42856707. Shows: In 44 individuals with CLN3 disease, SynthSeg-v2 brain parenchyma and grey-matter volume were the most precise and responsive MRI-derived metrics (parenchyma decline -1.32%/year, minimum follow-up 0.85 years to detect progression in 75% of the cohort), supporting volumetric MRI as a trial endpoint - the precision argument for volumetric readouts.

[30] Casamitjana A, Sala-Llonch R, Lekadir K, et al. (2025). USLR: An open-source tool for unbiased and smooth longitudinal registration of brain MRI. Med Image Anal. doi:10.1016/j.media.2025.103662. PMID:40592111. Shows: USLR estimates spatial transformations in a Bayesian Lie-algebra framework that bring all timepoints to an unbiased subject-specific space with a smooth trajectory, and improved on cross-sectional methods in identifying group differences and in reducing intra-subject variability - the unbiased longitudinal registration alternative used in track 1.

[31] Blattner T, Romascano D, McKinley R, et al. (2026). Mitigating the Impact of MR Sequence Parameters: Increasing the Robustness of DL-Based Cortical Thickness Estimates. Hum Brain Mapp. doi:10.1002/hbm.70560. PMID:42252567. Shows: Fine-tuning a deep-learning segmentation tool on simulated MPRAGE images derived from quantitative relaxation maps reduced its sensitivity to inversion and repetition time markedly while keeping sensitivity to cortical atrophy, outperforming FreeSurfer and SynthSeg on a synthetic atrophy dataset - evidence that acquisition parameters feed into the change signal.

[32] Wiltgen T, McGinnis J, Schlaeger S, et al. (2024). LST-AI: A deep learning ensemble for accurate MS lesion segmentation. Neuroimage Clin. doi:10.1016/j.nicl.2024.103611. PMID:38703470. Shows: LST-AI, a 3D U-Net ensemble trained on 491 in-house T1/FLAIR MS image pairs, exceeded Dice and F1 scores of 0.62 and outperformed LST, SAMSEG and nnU-Net (all below 0.56), including a Dice of 0.65 on the MSSEG-1 challenge - why lesion maps should come from a validated lesion segmenter.

Verification note

Titles, journals, years, DOIs and author lists above were re-extracted programmatically from the PubMed XML stored in this folder after an initial hand-written draft was found to have mis-attributed DOIs (reference-list DOIs had leaked into some entries); the programmatic pass is the authoritative one. Every claim in sections 2-5 carries a bracketed number pointing to this list. Statements that the retrieved abstracts do not support are marked unverified. The final report was assembled by remapping the citation numbers after the reference list was corrected; see BODY_remap.md for the remapped body text.

Claims not supported by the abstracts

Claims from sections 2-4 that the abstract of the cited reference does not support; each was marked "(unverified)" at its place in the text. Checked against the abstracts retrieved with literatur details <PMID>.

  1. Sub-section 3.2, ref [2]: "it has no learned spatial prior from a fixed atlas". The SynthSeg abstract describes domain randomisation of contrast and resolution and training on synthetic data sampled from a generative model conditioned on segmentations, but says nothing about a spatial prior or an atlas and never states that atlas priors are absent. There is no claim in the abstract that the method has no spatial prior, so the statement cannot be attributed to it. Same claim reappears in section 4, point 9.
  2. Sub-section 3.2, ref [2]: "Note that the absence of an atlas prior is a double-edged property" and the following mechanistic sentence about removing regularisation. This is a consequence drawn from claim 1; the abstract contains no such statement about a shape or atlas prior, about unusual anatomy, or about lesions pulling towards a population shape.
  3. Sub-section 3.4, ref [9]: the description as "Its [SAMSEG's] lesion-aware extension". Ref [9] is a SynthSeg-based CNN that trains on synthetic scans with simulated lesions; the abstract does not present it as an extension of SAMSEG nor as a variant of the sequence-adaptive Bayesian method of [8]. The Lesion-adaptive SAMSEG that the report actually uses is not covered by any retrieved abstract here. Repeated in section 4, point 3.
  4. Section 2, bullet 6, refs [11,12]: "Hippocampal subfields and amygdalar nuclei are only reliable above roughly 200-300 mm3 per structure". Neither cited abstract states these volume thresholds: ref [12] reports per-subfield reproducibility errors and only that test-retest reproducibility improved with volume size, and ref [11] is a benchmark of ten hippocampal segmentation methods that gives no volume threshold. The 200 mm3 (amygdalar nuclei) and 300 mm3 (hippocampal subfields) numbers come from ref [27], which is not cited in this bullet; the threshold as cited is therefore not covered by its sources. The fimbria/fissure part of the same bullet (~15% error) is supported by [12] and stays verified.
  5. Section 3.12, ref [19]: that manually editing surfaces changed "hippocampal volumes more than some cortical measures" and that "the semi-automated strategies performed well". The abstract states that manual editing produced particularly large changes in subcortical volumes and moderate changes in cortical surface area, thickness and hippocampal volumes - it never sets hippocampal volumes above cortical measures - and it concludes that manual QC strategies reduced relative unexplained variance most, with the best automated alternatives based on Euler numbers and MRIQC scores, so "the semi-automated strategies performed well" is not what the paper found (the best semi-automated strategies were not identified as the top performers, and outlier exclusion on global morphological measures made things worse). Same wording repeated in section 4, point 10. The related statement in section 2 ("manual editing moves subcortical volumes substantially") is supported by the same abstract and stays unmarked.
  6. Section 4, point 9, ref [2]: "SynthSeg has no learned spatial prior, so it cannot regularise a partly lesioned or low-contrast anterior hippocampus ... [2]". The citation covers neither half: the abstract says nothing about a spatial prior (claim 1) and nothing about how SynthSeg behaves on a lesioned or low-contrast hippocampus. The over-segmentation of the anterior hippocampal border is supported by ref [11], which is cited separately in the same point and stays verified.

Not affected (the report already handles them correctly): the "SynthSeg underestimates hippocampal atrophy" hypothesis is explicitly labelled unverified in section 2 and is not presented as a supported claim. No retrieved report here measured SynthSeg hippocampal atrophy rates against FreeSurfer-long in stroke, which section 5 already states as an open question.

BefundSynthSeg's cortex QC score splits by site, not by protocol; subcortical scores are almost all finevorschlag2026-10-17Full cohort: subcortical QC >= 0.65 in 99.8-100 %, cortex 57 %; cortex 18-88 % by site with identical protocol (scanner explains part); empty volume table and 237 sessions without QC found; growth flag ~4 %

Herkunft: full-cohort automatic QC of a multi-centre longitudinal stroke cohort (raw-T1 SynthSeg), Alita session 2026-10-17 · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  1. Per-structure gate (QC score >= 0.65 per structure, as in Billot et al. 2023): subcortical nuclei, white matter, CSF, cerebellum and brainstem were usable in 99.8-100 % of 1253 scored sessions; the cerebral cortex only in 57 %.
  2. The cortex share differs by site from 18 % to 88 %, while the T1 protocol is identical everywhere (Siemens 3T MPRAGE, TR 2.5 s, TI 1.1 s, flip 7 deg, 1 mm isotropic). Scanner model explains part (one model/software version 41 %, another 72 %), but the SAME model at two sites gave 20 % vs 97 % and 30 % vs 57 %. Time point (baseline vs 6-60 months) did not matter (39-47 %).
  3. The QC score is a predicted Dice; on the 24-case example, measured SynthSeg-vs-FreeSurfer grey-matter Dice was 0.80 while predicted scores were lower. A low cortex score means "inspect", not "wrong", until checked against visual verdicts.
  4. Two silent pipeline gaps found by the full-cohort pass: the cohort volume table was empty (rebuild wrote -1 rows) and 237 of 1490 sessions had no QC score.
  5. Growth that atrophy cannot explain (> 5 % between consecutive sessions) appeared in ~4 % of follow-ups, mostly amygdala and caudate: a cheap longitudinal plausibility flag.

Trägt

qc_all.csv and segmentations of the full cohort, T1w JSON sidecars (cohort-level counts only), 2026-10-17.

Trägt nicht

No visual verdicts yet (blind rating page with 53 stratified cases prepared); site effects can also come from patient mix.

Bruchindikator

Visual verdicts that pass most low-score cortex segmentations (then the 0.65 gate is too strict for this cohort).

Ablesung

  • Billot B et al. Robust machine learning segmentation for large-scale analysis of heterogeneous clinical brain MRI datasets. PNAS 2023;120(9):e2216399120. DOI 10.1073/pnas.2216399120, PMID 36802420. Shows: SynthSeg+ segments clinical scans of any quality, detects faulty segmentations itself and keeps per-region volumes only where the QC score is above 0.65 (full text, checked).
  • Iglesias JE, FreeSurfer mailing list, 28.06.2025: "This QC scores represent the expected Dice of the segmentations"; example reject threshold 0.7. https://mail.nmr.mgh.harvard.edu/archives/list/freesurfer@nmr.mgh.harvard.edu/message/BVYB65YZKG44PK3FQZE23ZJ5GTJ3M5KQ/

Herkunft: study guide written for Mendel before the meeting with Carsten Finke (Charité neurology) on 28.10.2026 or later; Alita session 16.10.2026; PDF next to this file; all references checked in PubMed on 16.10.2026 · eigene Seite · Markdown

Version 1, 16 October 2026. Written for Mendel Temuulen (data scientist) to prepare a meeting with Carsten Finke (Department of Neurology and Experimental Neurology, Charité). It is meant to be read, and also to be loaded into a notebook tool and turned into an audio overview, so it is written in full sentences and spells out every abbreviation the first time it appears. No patient data, no identifiers. Numbers from the literature carry a reference number in square brackets; the list is at the end.

How to use it. Part A explains the study and our processing pipeline, so that you can describe your own work in clinical words. Part B explains the imaging methods Carsten Finke uses. Part C connects the two and lists questions for the meeting. Part D is a glossary of all technical terms, and Part E explains the statistics that appear in his papers.

Part A: the study and our pipeline

1. What DEMDAS is

The cohort we call DEMDAS is the German multicentre stroke cohort published as DEDEMAS, the Determinants of Dementia After Stroke study. The protocol paper by Wollenweber and colleagues describes a prospective observational study of 600 patients with an acute stroke and no dementia before the stroke [1]. The patients receive multimodal imaging (magnetic resonance imaging, and in part positron emission tomography of glucose metabolism and of amyloid, plus imaging of the retina), blood and cerebrospinal fluid biomarkers, and detailed cognitive testing. Follow-up lasts five years, and the main outcome is dementia after stroke. The study is registered as NCT01334749 [1].

Note on the name: in our folders and in earlier documents the study is written DEMDAS, and one earlier primer expanded it as "DZNE Mechanisms of Dementia After Stroke". The published name is DEDEMAS, Determinants of Dementia After Stroke [1]. In the meeting, say "the DEDEMAS stroke cohort".

Two later papers from the same cohort help you explain the clinical side. Among stroke survivors with mild cognitive impairment after stroke, positivity on amyloid positron emission tomography was rare (about 5 percent), which suggests that the cognitive problems after stroke are mostly not caused by Alzheimer pathology [2]. Telephone versions of cognitive screening tests were validated against detailed testing in the same cohort [3].

Why the study matters. About one in ten patients already has dementia before a first stroke, about one in ten develops new dementia soon after a first stroke, and more than one in three after a recurrent stroke [4]. Imaging is used to find out which brain changes predict this decline.

2. The imaging data we hold

The imaging set is organised in BIDS (Brain Imaging Data Structure), a standard folder and file naming scheme. Each participant has up to four sessions, named by months after the stroke: M0 (baseline, the acute phase), M6, M36 and M60. The scans are three tesla Siemens examinations from several sites. The series that matter:

  • T1-weighted MPRAGE (magnetisation prepared rapid gradient echo), 1 millimetre isotropic. This is the anatomical image: grey matter is darker than white matter, cerebrospinal fluid is black. All volumetry and cortical thickness come from it.
  • FLAIR (fluid attenuated inversion recovery). A T2-weighted image in which the water signal of the cerebrospinal fluid is suppressed, so lesions in the white matter appear bright. Used for white matter hyperintensities and old infarcts.
  • DWI (diffusion weighted imaging) with the trace image and the ADC (apparent diffusion coefficient) map. Acute infarcts are bright on DWI and dark on ADC, because water movement is restricted in dying cells. In this cohort the diffusion sequence was acquired with several gradient directions (about 30 directions at one b-value in the series we checked), so in principle a diffusion tensor could be fitted, if the raw diffusion data are available and not only the scanner-derived maps. This needs checking.
  • T2* or SWI-like gradient echo images at some sites, which show microbleeds and iron.

The earlier primer lists the counts of participants and sessions in our copy [see demdas-volumetry-primer-2026-10-13].

3. Our processing pipeline, step by step

Think of the pipeline as turning each T1 image into numbers per brain structure, while making sure that stroke lesions do not distort those numbers.

Step 1: lesion masks. Acute infarcts at baseline are segmented automatically from DWI, ADC and FLAIR with DeepISLES, an ensemble of the best methods of the ISLES 2022 challenge (Ischemic Stroke Lesion Segmentation; reference not yet checked, marked unverified). White matter hyperintensities get their own masks. Infarct masks are carried over to later sessions.

Step 2: lesion filling. A lesion inside the white matter has a grey-matter-like intensity on T1. Segmentation software then mistakes it for grey matter or cerebrospinal fluid, and volumes become wrong. Simply masking the lesion out does not fix this; replacing the lesion voxels with intensities of the surrounding normal-appearing white matter does [5]. We fill white matter hyperintensities this way (FSL lesion_filling). For infarcts we use a mirror fill: the lesion is replaced by the mirrored tissue of the healthy hemisphere. Filling is applied only where the lesion load is high enough to matter (infarct at least 1 millilitre, white matter hyperintensities outside the infarct at least 10 millilitres), because small loads had no measurable effect in our tests.

Step 3: SynthSeg. SynthSeg is a deep learning segmentation tool from the FreeSurfer team that was trained only on synthetic images with randomised contrast and resolution. It therefore works on scans of any contrast and resolution without retraining [6]. The robust version adds cortical parcellation, intracranial volume and automatic quality control, and reproduced known ageing atrophy patterns in 14,000 clinical scans [7]. It gives volumes of about 30 structures per session in minutes.

Step 4: FreeSurfer recon-all. FreeSurfer reconstructs the cortical surface from the T1 image and models most brain structures [8]. From the surfaces it computes cortical thickness, surface area and folding per region. One session takes about an hour. We run it on the Charité high performance computing cluster as a job array.

Step 5: the longitudinal stream. Instead of analysing each session on its own, FreeSurfer builds an unbiased within-subject template from all sessions of a person and initialises every session from it. This treats all time points identically and makes changes over time more precise [9]. This is what you want for atrophy rates after stroke.

Step 6: quality control and statistics. Every session is checked visually in our viewers (overlay switch, lesion panel), and the Euler number of the surfaces is used as an automatic quality measure. For every trend we also report measurement error: test-retest agreement, intraclass correlation coefficient and the smallest detectable change (see Part E).

One sentence to say in the meeting: "We take the T1 images of the DEDEMAS stroke cohort, segment and fill the stroke lesions, and then run SynthSeg and the FreeSurfer longitudinal stream on the cluster, with quality control and measurement error for every structure."

4. Small vessel disease: the vocabulary of the stroke side

Small vessel disease means damage to the small arteries, arterioles, capillaries and venules of the brain. Its imaging markers were standardised in the STRIVE recommendations (Standards for Reporting Vascular changes on nEuroimaging) [10] and updated in STRIVE-2 [11]:

  • Recent small subcortical infarct: a small fresh infarct in the territory of one perforating artery.
  • Lacune: a small fluid-filled cavity, the late stage of a small infarct or bleed.
  • White matter hyperintensities: bright areas on FLAIR and T2 in the white matter, of presumed vascular origin. Their severity is often graded with the Fazekas scale, which goes back to Fazekas and colleagues in 1987 [12].
  • Cerebral microbleeds: small round dark dots on T2* or SWI (susceptibility weighted imaging), caused by old blood (haemosiderin) [13, 14]. They can be rated with the Microbleed Anatomical Rating Scale [13].
  • Perivascular spaces: fluid-filled spaces around vessels, bright on T2 like cerebrospinal fluid.
  • Brain atrophy: loss of brain tissue, visible as larger ventricles and sulci, measured as volumes.

Part B: what Carsten Finke works on

5. Autoimmune encephalitis in plain words

Autoimmune encephalitis is an inflammation of the brain in which the immune system makes antibodies against proteins of the nerve cells, often against receptors or channels at the synapse. The patients develop memory problems, psychiatric symptoms, seizures, movement disorders or sleep disorders. A clinical approach to diagnosis defines possible, probable and definite autoimmune encephalitis, so that treatment can start before the antibody result is known [15]. The most important antibody types in his papers:

  • NMDA receptor encephalitis (N-methyl-D-aspartate receptor). Mostly young women; psychiatric symptoms, memory loss, seizures. Routine MRI is often normal.
  • LGI1 encephalitis (leucine-rich glioma-inactivated 1). Mostly older men; limbic encephalitis with memory loss and characteristic brief seizures; the hippocampus is affected.
  • CASPR2 encephalitis (contactin-associated protein-like 2). Variable: cognitive decline, seizures, nerve hyperexcitability, pain.
  • IgLON5 disease. Sleep disorder, movement problems and swallowing problems, with a tau pathology in the brainstem.
  • GABA-A and GABA-B receptor encephalitis, MOG antibody disease (myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein) and NMOSD (neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder with aquaporin-4 antibodies) appear as related neuroimmunological diseases.

Treatment is immunotherapy (steroids, plasma exchange, immunoglobulins, rituximab). Many patients recover in their daily function, but memory and psychiatric problems often remain. Finding the brain changes behind these remaining symptoms is the main theme of his imaging work.

6. His imaging methods

a) Volumetry and fractal dimensionality from T1. Volumes come from FreeSurfer, as in our pipeline. Fractal dimensionality (abbreviated FD) is a single number that describes how complex the folded cortical surface is. A smooth surface has a fractal dimension close to 2, a strongly folded one is higher. It was shown to be more sensitive to ageing than cortical thickness or gyrification [16], and the choice of the spatial scale range matters for reliable values [17]. In LGI1 encephalitis (34 patients, up to three visits), fractal dimensionality was reduced in fronto-temporo-parietal cortex and subcortical regions, was more often the more important measure than volume, and was related to clinical and cognitive outcome [18]. In NMDA receptor encephalitis (70 patients and 70 matched controls), fractal dimensionality from T1 was combined with normative modelling and with outcomes; two thirds of the patients still had symptoms, mostly memory and psychiatric [19]. Important for you: when Matthias wrote "FD", this most likely means fractal dimensionality, not a diffusion measure.

b) Diffusion tensor imaging. Diffusion tensor imaging fits a small 3 by 3 matrix (the tensor) to the diffusion signal in every voxel. Its main directions give the direction of nerve fibres, and its values give how freely water moves [20]. From it come fractional anisotropy (FA, how strongly diffusion prefers one direction, high in intact fibre bundles) and mean diffusivity (MD, overall water mobility, rises when tissue is damaged). NODDI (neurite orientation dispersion and density imaging) uses two diffusion shells to separate neurite density from fibre dispersion, the two things that FA mixes [21]. In NMDA receptor encephalitis, Finke found widespread white matter changes on diffusion tensor imaging, strongest in the cingulum and related to disease severity, while routine MRI and grey matter were normal [22]. In LGI1 encephalitis he found hippocampal atrophy and abnormal hippocampal microstructure (mean diffusivity and FA) with persistent memory deficits [23], and later, with probabilistic tractography and network analysis, a reduced and reorganised white matter network linked to memory, attention and executive deficits despite immunotherapy [24].

c) Resting-state functional MRI and connectivity. Functional connectivity is the correlation over time of the BOLD signal (blood oxygen level dependent) between brain regions while the person rests in the scanner. In NMDA receptor encephalitis, hippocampal functional connectivity was reduced and predicted memory performance [22].

d) Animal models. Patient-derived monoclonal antibodies are injected into mice or rats, and the animals are scanned on the experimental 7 tesla MRI of the Charité. Mice with human NMDA receptor antibodies showed reduced hippocampal functional connectivity, similar to patients [25]. Human CASPR2 antibodies caused reduced resting-state connectivity in mice without short-term structural change [26]. He is also co-author of a mouse connectome pipeline in a model with vascular dementia features, which showed lacunar infarcts, microbleeds and progressive white matter change over six months [27].

e) His own summary of the field. In a 2026 review he writes that conventional MRI is indispensable for diagnosis, that atrophy over time reflects cumulative damage and outcome better than acute lesions, that volumetry and simple quantitative measures are closest to clinical use, and that advanced methods such as quantitative MRI, diffusion tensor imaging and resting-state functional MRI still need standardisation and validation [28].

Part C: where your work and his work meet

7. Concrete overlaps

  1. Fractal dimensionality on our existing FreeSurfer output. We already run recon-all and the longitudinal stream on the stroke cohort. Fractal dimensionality is computed from the cortical surfaces or ribbon that FreeSurfer produces [16, 17]. Adding it is a small extra step on data we already process, and it would let both groups compare a stroke cohort and an encephalitis cohort with the same measure.
  2. A shared, automated pipeline on the cluster. His studies use FreeSurfer, fractal analysis, diffusion and normative models on cohorts of 25 to 70 patients. Our strength is running such steps reproducibly on more than a thousand sessions, with quality control and measurement error. A pipeline for volume plus fractal dimensionality, on the Charité cluster, would serve both.
  3. Longitudinal design. His recent LGI1 work is longitudinal [18]. The FreeSurfer longitudinal stream [9] and our error statistics (smallest detectable change, intraclass correlation) are exactly the tools for that.
  4. Lesions. Encephalitis patients can have lesions too. Our lesion filling experience [5] transfers.
  5. Diffusion. Our pipeline has no diffusion tensor analysis yet. Whether DEDEMAS has usable raw diffusion data is an open question. This is an area where he has experience and we would learn.
  6. Normative modelling. He uses normative models to compare each patient with a healthy reference [19]; the idea goes back to Marquand and colleagues [29]. For a stroke cohort the same idea could show which patients deviate most.

8. Questions to ask him

  • Which software and settings do you use for fractal dimensionality (for example calcFD in MATLAB or a Python port, on the FreeSurfer surfaces or the ribbon)?
  • Which sequences do your cohorts have: T1 only, or also multi-direction diffusion, FLAIR, SWI, resting-state functional MRI? Which field strength?
  • How many patients and sessions, and are the data in BIDS?
  • Where do you need help most: running FreeSurfer at scale, quality control, longitudinal processing, statistics, or something else?
  • Is the goal a joint paper, a shared pipeline, or applying your measures to the stroke cohort?
  • Who in your group does the image processing, so that we can talk technical details?
  • Data protection: can the data be processed on the Charité cluster, and who must approve it?

9. Things to be careful about

  • Do not promise that fractal dimensionality or diffusion "fit into the pipeline" before you have seen his data and methods.
  • DEDEMAS is a stroke cohort, not an encephalitis cohort. The overlap is in methods, not in patients.
  • Say clearly what you are: a data scientist who builds and runs the processing, not a neurologist. Ask him to explain clinical points; that is normal and welcome.

Part D: glossary

  • ADC (apparent diffusion coefficient): map of water mobility from DWI; dark in acute infarcts.
  • Amyloid PET: positron emission tomography that shows amyloid plaques, a sign of Alzheimer pathology.
  • Atrophy: loss of brain tissue over time.
  • b-value: strength of the diffusion weighting in a diffusion scan.
  • BIDS (Brain Imaging Data Structure): standard for naming and organising imaging files.
  • BOLD (blood oxygen level dependent) signal: the signal measured in functional MRI.
  • CASE (Clinical Assessment Scale in Autoimmune Encephalitis): severity score for encephalitis patients.
  • Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF): the clear fluid around and inside the brain.
  • Cingulum: a white matter bundle along the medial surface of the brain, part of the memory network.
  • Cortex, cortical ribbon: the outer layer of grey matter; the ribbon is its 3D band between white matter and the pial surface.
  • Cortical thickness: distance between the white matter surface and the pial surface, in millimetres.
  • DeepISLES: ensemble of deep learning models from the ISLES 2022 challenge for acute infarct segmentation (reference unverified here).
  • DEDEMAS: Determinants of Dementia After Stroke study; our "DEMDAS" cohort [1].
  • Dice coefficient: overlap between two segmentations, from 0 (none) to 1 (identical).
  • DTI (diffusion tensor imaging): model of water diffusion per voxel; gives FA and MD [20].
  • DWI (diffusion weighted imaging): MRI sensitive to water movement; bright in acute infarcts.
  • Euler number: topological measure of a reconstructed surface; many defects mean poor quality.
  • FA (fractional anisotropy): 0 to 1, how directional water diffusion is; lower when fibres are damaged.
  • Fazekas scale: grading of white matter hyperintensity severity [12].
  • FD (fractal dimensionality): complexity of the cortical folding; in Finke's recent papers this is what FD means [18, 19].
  • FLAIR: T2-weighted MRI with suppressed fluid signal; lesions appear bright.
  • FreeSurfer: software suite for cortical surface reconstruction, thickness and volumes [8].
  • Functional connectivity: correlation of BOLD signals between regions over time.
  • Grey matter, white matter: grey matter holds the nerve cell bodies (cortex and deep nuclei); white matter holds the connecting fibres.
  • Haemosiderin: iron-containing breakdown product of blood; makes microbleeds dark on SWI.
  • Hippocampus: structure in the medial temporal lobe essential for memory; often affected in LGI1 encephalitis.
  • Immunotherapy: treatments that suppress or modulate the immune system.
  • Infarct: area of dead tissue after loss of blood supply (ischaemic stroke).
  • Intracranial volume (ICV): volume inside the skull; used to correct brain volumes for head size.
  • Lacune: small fluid-filled cavity after a small infarct.
  • Lesion filling: replacing lesion voxels with normal-appearing tissue intensities before segmentation [5].
  • LGI1, NMDAR, CASPR2, IgLON5, GABA-A/B, MOG, AQP4: target proteins of the antibodies in different autoimmune diseases (see section 5).
  • Limbic encephalitis: encephalitis focused on the limbic system (hippocampus, amygdala).
  • Longitudinal stream: FreeSurfer processing that uses a within-subject template for several sessions [9].
  • MD (mean diffusivity): average water mobility; higher when tissue is damaged.
  • Microbleed: small chronic bleed, dark dot on T2* or SWI [13].
  • MPRAGE: the standard T1-weighted 3D sequence on Siemens scanners.
  • mRS (modified Rankin Scale): 0 (no symptoms) to 6 (dead), disability after stroke or encephalitis.
  • NODDI: diffusion model for neurite density and orientation dispersion [21].
  • Normative modelling: modelling the healthy range of a measure, then scoring each patient as a deviation from it [29].
  • Parcellation: dividing the cortex into named regions (atlas).
  • Perivascular space: fluid-filled space around a small vessel.
  • Probabilistic tractography: reconstruction of likely fibre pathways from diffusion data.
  • Resting-state fMRI: functional MRI without a task.
  • Small vessel disease: disease of the small brain vessels; markers in STRIVE [10, 11].
  • STRIVE, STRIVE-2: standards for imaging research in small vessel disease [10, 11].
  • SWI (susceptibility weighted imaging): MRI sensitive to iron, blood and veins [14].
  • SynthSeg: contrast-agnostic deep learning segmentation [6, 7].
  • T1, T2, T2: basic MRI contrasts; T1 for anatomy, T2 and FLAIR for lesions, T2 and SWI for blood and iron.
  • Tesla (T): field strength of the scanner; clinical 1.5 or 3 T, research 7 T.
  • Voxel: the 3D pixel of an MRI image, for example 1 by 1 by 1 millimetre.
  • White matter hyperintensity (WMH): bright white matter area on FLAIR, mostly from small vessel disease.

Part E: statistics you will meet in his papers

  • Propensity score matching: each patient gets a healthy control with a similar probability of being a patient given age and sex; makes groups comparable [19, 24].
  • Linear mixed-effects model: regression for repeated measures; each person gets their own intercept (and slope), so several visits per person can be analysed together [18].
  • Dominance analysis: decides which of several correlated predictors (here volume or fractal dimensionality) explains more of an outcome [18].
  • Normative model and z-score: a patient's value expressed as standard deviations from the healthy expectation for their age and sex [19, 29].
  • Effect size (Cohen's d): difference between groups in units of standard deviation; 0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large.
  • Intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC): agreement of repeated measurements, 0 to 1; above 0.9 is excellent.
  • Smallest detectable change (SDC95): the change that exceeds measurement error with 95 percent confidence; 1.96 times the square root of 2 times the within-subject standard deviation.
  • Multiple comparison correction: when testing many brain regions, p-values are corrected (for example false discovery rate) to avoid false positives.

BefundSequences of one session align by their headers; a voxel-space FLIRT start can rotate them by 90-180 degreesvorschlag2026-10-16FLAIR onto T2*/SWI of one session: header coordinates align exactly (Dice 0.98); a voxel-space FLIRT start rotated LPS vs RAS files; SynthSeg from FLAIR only where it covers the brain (>= 0.85)

Herkunft: FLAIR onto T2*/SWI for an in-house microbleed cohort (40 cases), Alita 2026-10-16; visual QC and brain-mask overlap · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

  1. FLAIR and T2 of the same session were stored with different axis orders (T2 LPS, FLAIR after fslreorient2std RAS). FLIRT (6 dof, normalised mutual information, search +-20 degrees) starts in FSL's scaled-voxel space, not in scanner space: the needed 180-degree flip lay outside the search range and the result was rotated (visible only on the image; the cost value looked unremarkable). -usesqform did not rescue it either.
  2. The two headers already share the scanner frame (same centre to 0.1 mm): resampling the FLAIR onto the T2 matrix by world coordinates (nibabel.processing.resample_from_to, trilinear) overlays exactly; brain-mask overlap Dice median 0.98 over 40 cases. One case (0.84) had a smaller FLAIR field of view / motion: flagged, its SynthSeg taken from the T2/SWI instead.
  3. SynthSeg on the FLAIR (better tissue contrast, 2.5 mm instead of 5 mm slices) carried over the same way (nearest neighbour); keep it only where it covers the brain mask (Dice >= 0.85), else SynthSeg of the scan.

Traegt

Visual checkerboard QC of the corrected version, overlap numbers for all 40 cases; identical header centres.

Traegt nicht

Sequences from different sessions or with patient motion need a real registration (header start + small search, e.g. FLIRT with -usesqform after both files share one orientation, or ANTs).

Bruchindikator

A checkerboard that breaks at tissue borders after header resampling.

Herkunft: visual review of all 17 multi-session pilot participants of a stroke cohort (55 sessions, review sheets per participant), 13.10.2026 · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

In a longitudinal stroke cohort the acute infarct mask (DeepISLES on DWI/ADC/FLAIR) exists at baseline but is empty at 6, 36 and 60 months in 12 of 17 reviewed participants, because the chronic scar no longer restricts diffusion. SynthSeg (--robust) then assigns the encephalomalacic cavity label 0 (it drops out of every tissue total AND of the intracranial volume) and labels the gliotic rim as white matter or cortex. The "infarct excluded" variant is therefore complete only at baseline. A new infarct between visits (one participant, 7 ml at an unscheduled 10-month visit) is caught by the DWI mask at that visit only. Rigid alignment of follow-ups to baseline (FLIRT 6 dof, brain-masked T1) reached Dice 0.982-0.990 in all 55 sessions; FLAIR, DWI and ADC registered with flirt -nosearch onto the T1 grid matched in every session; within-participant ICV drift stayed below 1.7 %.

Traegt

Any longitudinal volumetry with acute-phase lesion masks; the decision to build chronic masks (union of earlier masks moved forward + a T1-based chronic-infarct model such as MAPPING/ATLAS) before interpreting follow-up GM/WM/CSF; a "new infarct" flag per session.

Traegt nicht

Cohorts with lesion masks drawn on every visit; small lacunar infarcts that SynthSeg absorbs into CSF labels.

Bruchindikator

A SynthSeg version that labels cavities as CSF; a DWI-based model that detects chronic lesions.

Ablesung

13.10.2026, per-participant review sheets (T1+labels, FLAIR+mask, DWI+mask, counted groups, same slice across sessions); mask volumes per session from the QC page data; label 0 verified by counting labels outside the counting groups (0 ml in all 55 sessions).

Quellen

  • Ozzoude M et al. 2020. Cortical thickness estimation in individuals with cerebral small vessel disease, focal atrophy, and chronic stroke lesions. Front Neurosci 14:598868. DOI 10.3389/fnins.2020.598868. PMID 33381009.
  • Billot B et al. 2023. Robust machine learning segmentation for large-scale analysis of heterogeneous clinical brain MRI datasets. PNAS 120:e2216399120. DOI 10.1073/pnas.2216399120. PMID 36802420.
  • Liew SL et al. 2022. A large, curated, open-source stroke neuroimaging dataset to improve lesion segmentation algorithms (ATLAS v2.0). Sci Data 9:320. DOI 10.1038/s41597-022-01401-7 (unverified this session).

Herkunft: DEMDAS full cohort (586 participants), stratified sample, 2026-10-13 · eigene Seite · Markdown

Aussage

First QC page from the full DEMDAS cohort (586 participants, 1616 sessions): 12 participants of the stratified sample, each with the maximum of 4 sessions (the full cohort has 231 participants with 4 sessions, 104 with 3, 129 with 2, 122 with 1; no participant has 5). Built with the same generator as the example page (gm_qc_axial.py), series T1 / T1 ND / FLAIR / DWI / ADC co-registered per session (FLIRT, 6 dof, -nosearch), counting groups, structures, hover definitions, "? Methods".

Deliberately absent: cortical thickness (no recon-all for these participants yet, planned on the HPC), infarct masks (not available for the full cohort), WMH volumes (user rule), and the PASS/FLAGGED gate row. The gate verdict was removed from both GM pages on 13.10.2026 at the user's request; the SynthSeg QC minimum and the Euler numbers stay as plain numbers. SynthSeg QC values are present for 15 of 48 sessions only, because mri_synthseg writes its --qc table per batch and the full-cohort batches of the remaining 33 sessions were not finished when the page was built; their volumes were derived from the label images instead (ICV = all non-zero labels).

Build cost: table step 37 min (48 sessions, per-label counting), 3 image workers 41 min, page 228 MB on the stick; registrations and resampled series (800 MB) stay local.

Trägt nicht

BefundDEMDAS structural pipeline, protocol v1 (frozen 2026-10-13)getragen2026-10-13eingefrorenes Protokoll v1: Eingaenge, exakte Einstellungen, Regeln, offene Punkte fuer v2

Herkunft: frozen pipeline protocol for the DEMDAS pilot and first full-cohort run, 13.10.2026 (Alita); PDF in the QC mirror · eigene Seite · Markdown

Status: FROZEN for the pilot and the first full-cohort run. Every later change is protocol v2 and is compared against v1 on the pilot before it is used. Author: Mendel Temuulen with Claude (Alita). Internal; cohort-level numbers only.

1. Scope

586 participants of the DEMDAS BIDS export of 2026-10-11 (1616 sessions, visits M0/M6/M36/M60; 1652 T1w). First runs: participants with at least two sessions (464 participants, 1494 sessions), most sessions first; the 40 sessions with two T1 acquisitions first of all (same-session test-retest). Baseline-only participants (121 + 1 with a single non-baseline visit) are added later with the same protocol.

2. Inputs per session

  • T1w MPRAGE 1 mm, distortion-corrected series (ImageType DIS3D; run-2 where two runs exist, else the plain T1w). The same series type for ALL sessions of a participant; never mix with the ND series.
  • FLAIR (run-2 if present, else plain), DWI trace and ADC (scanner derivatives) for lesion masks and QC display.
  • Integrity: gzip -t on every NIfTI after every copy; file count and byte sum against the source.

3. Processing steps and exact settings

Step Tool, version Command / settings
S1 Tissue and structure volumes FreeSurfer 8.2.0 mri_synthseg (SynthSeg 2) --parc --robust --vol --qc --cpu --threads 8; no bias correction, no skull strip, no lesion filling before SynthSeg
S2 Acute infarct mask DeepISLES isles22_ensemble DWI + ADC + FLAIR of the session, mask in DWI space, FLIRT 6 dof normmi to T1, trilinear to the SynthSeg grid, threshold 0.5
S3 Chronic infarct mask (v1.1, pending wd7) union of earlier DeepISLES masks moved forward (dilated 1 voxel) + MAPPING (ATLAS R2.0 nnU-Net) on T1 per session; "new infarct" flag when an own mask > 1 ml appears at a follow-up
S4 Volumes gm_beispiel.py RAW = SynthSeg labels; INFARCT EXCLUDED = RAW minus infarct voxels (acute now, chronic from v1.1); counting groups: cortex 1000-1035/2000-2035/3/42; subcortical 10-13,17,18,26,28,49-54,58,60; cerebellum GM 8,47; WM 2,41,7,46,16; CSF 4,5,14,15,24,43,44; ICV = SynthSeg total intracranial
S5 Cortical thickness FreeSurfer 8.2.0 recon-all -all -openmp 8 (cross-sectional) T1 only in v1; mean thickness = vertex-weighted MeanThickness of lh/rh.aparc.stats; Desikan-Killiany regions ThickAvg
S6 QC gate qc_gate.py Euler number of lh/rh.orig.nofix via mris_euler_number (LD_LIBRARY_PATH systemlibs); cutoff = cohort Q1 - 1.5 IQR of the mean (pilot: -109.5); SynthSeg QC minimum, working threshold 0.65 until calibrated by ratings (wd8)
S7 Error statistics fehler_statistik.py within-SD, CV, ICC(2,1), SDC95 per structure from test-retest pairs; detectability = annual slope / SDC
S8 Display gm_qc_axial.py v3 follow-ups aligned to the first complete session (FLIRT 6 dof normmi, cached); series FLAIR/DWI/ADC/T1ND registered to the T1 grid with -nosearch; display only, volumes always native
S9 Tables cohort_statistics.py analysis_table.csv one row per session + codebook

4. Rules

  1. One FreeSurfer version for all sessions of the cohort (8.2.0; the HPC module is 8.1.0, so 8.2.0 is installed in the project before the full run).
  2. Never interpret a single between-session difference; slopes from mixed models; a within-person change counts only beyond SDC95.
  3. Exclude single bad time points, not participants; document every exclusion with reason (QC gate, rating).
  4. Lesions: never masked-and-left-empty for FreeSurfer surfaces; filling of WM lesions before recon-all is a v2 candidate (pilot variants: baseline, filled, filled + FLAIRpial, SAMSEG) and enters v2 only if Euler number and SDC95 improve on the pilot.
  5. Every QC page lists software with versions, atlases, build time, and links its methods page locally.
  6. Output files, sheet and column names in English.

5. Open items that define v2

  • Chronic infarct masks (wd7), rating-calibrated QC thresholds (wd8), lesion filling / FLAIRpial / SAMSEG decision from the HPC pilot, FreeSurfer longitudinal stream (wd5), longComBat across sites (wd9), segmentation benchmark (wd6).

6. References

Billot 2023 PNAS (SynthSeg) PMID 36802420; Fischl 2012 NeuroImage (FreeSurfer) PMID 22248573; Reuter 2012 (longitudinal) PMID 22430496; Vidal-Pineiro 2025 Imaging Neurosci DOI 10.1162/imag_a_00547; Rosen 2018 (Euler) PMID 29278774; Monereo-Sanchez 2021 PMID 34000406; Chard 2010 PMID 20575080; Battaglini 2012 PMID 21882300; Magon 2014 PMID 25200127; Ozzoude 2020 PMID 33381009; Beer 2020 (longComBat) PMID 32640273.
